    {
    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning (BOIL), a novel Bayesian optimization (BO) approach designed for efficient hyperparameter tuning of deep (reinforcement) learning systems. It addresses the high computational cost and noise associated with iterative training by exploiting the learning algorithm's structure. Key contributions include: 1) an algorithm that optimizes the learning curve through training curve compression instead of relying on averaged final performance; 2) a method to learn this compression curve from data and an effective data augmentation technique for improved sample-efficiency; and 3) empirical demonstration of its efficiency in tuning hyperparameters for deep reinforcement learning agents and convolutional neural networks, outperforming existing baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time.",
    "Methodology": "BOIL frames hyperparameter tuning as a global optimization problem based on noisy evaluations of a black-box function f, modeling it with a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate over the joint space of hyperparameters x and training iterations t, using a product kernel k(x,x') × k(t,t'). The training time cost c(x,t) is approximated by a linear regressor. It uses a modified Expected Improvement (EI) acquisition function to select the next hyperparameter-iteration pair by balancing expected utility against predicted cost. A core technique is 'training curve compression', which transforms the raw learning curve into a single numeric score using a Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function. The parameters of this Sigmoid function (growth parameter g0 and middle point m0) are learned dynamically by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood. To improve sample-efficiency and prevent GP covariance matrix ill-conditioning, BOIL employs a selective data augmentation technique, adding a subset of points from observed training curves based on maximum GP predictive uncertainty, adaptively chosen to keep the log condition number of the covariance matrix below a threshold.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on two Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) agents and a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN). DRL tasks included a Dueling DQN (DDQN) agent on the CartPole-v0 environment and Advantage Actor Critic (A2C) agents on the InvertedPendulum-v2 and Reacher-v2 environments using OpenAI Gym and Mujoco. CNN tuning was performed on the SVHN and CIFAR10 datasets. All results were averaged over 20 independent runs with different random seeds, and final performance was assessed by evaluating chosen hyperparameters at maximum iterations. The system ran on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using tensorflow-gpu. BOIL used square-exponential kernels for its GP, with parameters optimized via marginal likelihood maximization. The data augmentation strategy involved a maximum of 15 augmented points and a threshold of 20 for the natural log of the GP covariance matrix condition number. Baselines for comparison included Hyperband and Continuous Multi-Task/Multi-Fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO).",
    "Limitations": "The cost function c(x,t) is approximated by a linear regressor, which might be overly simplistic and less appropriate if the true cost has a more complex dependency on hyperparameters and iterations. Furthermore, the inherent noisiness and unpredictable fluctuations of DRL reward curves pose a general challenge, making traditional methods relying on assumptions like exponential decay or simple early stopping criteria unsuitable. While BOIL actively addresses the issue of GP covariance matrix ill-conditioning from naive full-curve augmentation, careful management of augmentation is still required to maintain model stability.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work includes exploring the broader applicability of the BOIL framework to any process exhibiting an iterative structure beyond machine learning, such as optimizing manufacturing pipelines. Potential technical extensions could involve investigating more sophisticated models for approximating the training time cost c(x,t), exploring different acquisition functions within the flexible BOIL framework, and examining alternative kernel choices or adaptive kernel parameter estimation strategies to further enhance performance and robustness. More generally, the paper suggests reflecting on societal impacts, ensuring supervision and override mechanisms, and employing interpretability techniques as automated ML pipelines become more prevalent.",

var = gp.predict(x, eval_MSE=True)\n        var2 = np.maximum(var, 1e-10 + 0 * var)\n        z = (mean - y_max)/np.sqrt(var2)        \n        out=(mean - y_max) * norm.cdf(z) + np.sqrt(var2) * norm.pdf(z)\n        \n        out[var2<1e-10]=0\n        return out\n \n \n    @staticmethod      \n    def _poi(x, gp,y_max): # run Predictive Entropy Search using Spearmint\n        mean, var = norm.cdf(z)\n\n   \ndef unique_rows(a):\n    \"\"\"\n    A functions to trim repeated rows that may appear when optimizing.\n    This is necessary to avoid the sklearn GP object from breaking\n\n transform_logistic_marginal(curves,MaxEpisode=1000):\n    # curve is a matrix [nParameter x MaxIter]\n    # or curve is a vector [1 x MaxIter]\n\n    def transform_one_logistic_marginal(curves,MaxEpisode):\n        # curve is a vector [1 x MaxIter]\n    \n        midpoint_list=[-3,-2,-1,0,1]\n        growth_list=[0.1,1,2,3]\n        \n        temp_Y_value=[0]*(len(midpoint_list)*len(growth_list))\n        for idx, (val1, val2) in enumerate(itertools.product(midpoint_list,growth_list)):\n            temp_Y_value[idx]=apply_one_transform_logistic(curves,val1, val2,MaxEpisode)\n                \n        temp_Y_value=np.asarray(temp_Y_value)\n        \n        Y=np.mean(temp_Y_value,axis=0)\n        return Y\n    if len(curves)==1:\n        output=transform_one_logistic_marginal(curves[0],MaxEpisode)\n    else:\n        output=[0]*len(curves)\n        for idx, curve in enumerate(curves):\n            output[idx]=transform_one_logistic_marginal(curve,MaxEpisode)\n    return output    \n\n\ndef transform_logistic(curves, midpoint=0, growth=1,MaxEpisode=1000):\n    # curve is a matrix [nParameter x MaxIter]\n    # or curve is a vector [1 x MaxIter]\n\n    if len(curves)==1:\n        output=apply_one_transform_logistic(curves[0], midpoint, growth,MaxEpisode)\n    else:\n        output=[0]*len(curves)\n        for idx, curve in enumerate(curves):\n            output[idx]=apply_one_transform_logistic(curve, midpoint, growth,MaxEpisode)\n    return output\n    \n\n        \n    # -*- coding: utf-8 -*-\n# define Gaussian Process class\n\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom bayes_opt.acquisition_functions import unique_rows\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import MinMaxScaler\n\nfrom transform_logistic\n\n\nclass ProductGaussianProcess(object):\n    # in this class of Gaussian process, we define k( {x,t}, {x',t'} )= k(x,x')*k(t,t')\n    \n    \n    #def __init__ (self,param):\n    def __init__ (self,SearchSpace,gp_hyper=None,logistic_hyper=None,verbose=0):\n        self.noise_delta=5e-4\n        self.noise_upperbound=1e-2\n        self.mycov=self.cov_RBF_time\n        self.SearchSpace=SearchSpace\n        scaler = MinMaxScaler()\n        scaler.fit(SearchSpace.T)\n        self.Xscaler=scaler\n        self.verbose=verbose\n        self.dim=SearchSpace.shape[0]\n        \n        if gp_hyper is None:\n            self.hyper={}\n            self.hyper['var']=1 # standardise the data\n            self.hyper['lengthscale_x']=0.02 #to be optimised\n            self.hyper['lengthscale_t']=0.2 #to be optimised\n        else:\n            self.hyper=gp_hyper\n\n        \n        if logistic_hyper is None:\n            self.logistic_hyper={}\n            self.logistic_hyper['midpoint']=0.0\n            self.logistic_hyper['growth']=1.0   \n        else:\n            self.logistic_hyper=logistic_hyper\n\n        self.X=[]\n        self.T=[]\n        self.Y=[]\n        self.Y_curves=None\n#        self.hyper['lengthscale_x']_old=self.hyper['lengthscale_x']\n#        self.hyper['lengthscale_x']_old_t=self.hyper['lengthscale_x']_t\n        \n        self.alpha=[] # for Cholesky update\n        self.L=[] # for Cholesky update LL'=A\n        \n        self.MaxEpisode=0\n        \n        return None\n       \n\n    def cov_RBF_time(self, x1,t1,x2,t2,lengthscale,lengthscale_t):\n        \n        Euc_dist=euclidean_distances(x1,x2)\n        exp_dist_x=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/lengthscale)\n        \n        Euc_dist=euclidean_distances(t1,t2)\n        exp_dist_t=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/lengthscale_t)\n        \n        return exp_dist_x*exp_dist_t\n                \n    def fit(self,X,T,Y,Y_curves):\n        \"\"\"\n        Fit Gaussian Process model\n\n        Input Parameters\n        ----------\n        x: the observed points \n        t: time or number of episode\n        y: the outcome y=f(x)\n        \n        \"\"\" \n        temp=np.hstack((X,T))\n        ur = unique_rows(temp)\n        \n        T=T[ur]\n        X=X[ur]\n        Y=Y[ur]\n        \n        self.X=X\n        self.Y=Y\n        self.T=T\n        self.Y_curves=[val for idx,val in enumerate(Y_curves) if ur[idx]==True]\n        \n        for curves in self.Y_curves:\n            self.MaxEpisode=max(len(curves),self.MaxEpisode)\n        #self.Y_curves=Y_curves[myidx]\n            \n        Euc_dist_x=euclidean_distances(X,X)\n        #exp_dist_x=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x'])+np.eye(len(X))*self.noise_delta\n    \n        Euc_dist_t=euclidean_distances(T,T)\n        #exp_dist_t=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']_t)+np.eye(len(X))*self.noise_delta       \n    \n        self.KK_x_x=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']\\\n                           -np.square(Euc_dist_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])+np.eye(len(X))*self.noise_delta\n          \n        if np.isnan(self.KK_x_x).any(): #NaN\n            print(\"nan in KK_x_x\")\n        \n        #self.KK_x_x_inv=np.linalg.pinv(self.KK_x_x)\n        self.L=np.linalg.cholesky(self.KK_x_x)\n        temp=np.linalg.solve(self.L,self.Y)\n        self.alpha=np.linalg.solve(self.L.T,temp)\n        self.cond_num=self.compute_condition_number()\n        \n    def compute_condition_number(self):\n        cond_num=np.linalg.cond(self.KK_x_x)\n        return cond_num\n    \n\n    def log_marginal_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(self,hyper,noise_delta):\n        \"\"\"\n        Compute Log Marginal likelihood of the GP model w.r.t. the provided lengthscale, noise_delta and Logistic hyperparameter\n        \"\"\"\n\n        def compute_log_marginal_with_logistic_hyper(lengthscale, lengthscale_t,midpoint,growth,noise_delta):\n            # compute K\n            temp=np.hstack((self.X,self.T))\n            ur = unique_rows(temp)\n            myX=self.X[ur]\n            myT=self.T[ur]\n            \n            # transform Y_curve to Y_original, then to Y\n            Y_original=transform_logistic(self.Y_curves,midpoint,growth,self.MaxEpisode)\n            myY=(Y_original-np.mean(Y_original))/np.std(Y_original)\n            \n            myY=myY[ur]\n          \n            self.Euc_dist_x=euclidean_distances(myX,myX)\n            self.Euc_dist_t=euclidean_distances(myT,myT)\n        \n            KK=np.exp(-np.square(self.Euc_dist_x)/lengthscale-np.square(self.Euc_dist_t)/lengthscale_t)\n                +np.eye(len(myX))*noise_delta\n                    \n            \n            try:\n                temp_inv=np.linalg.solve(KK,myY)\n            except: # singular\n                return -np.inf\n            \n            try:\n                #logmarginal=-0.5*np.dot(self.Y.T,temp_inv)-0.5*np.log(np.linalg.det(KK+noise_delta))-0.5*len(X)*np.log(2*3.14)\n                first_term=-0.5*np.dot(myY.T,temp_inv)\n                \n                # if the matrix is too large, we randomly select a part of the data for fast computation\n                if KK.shape[0]>200:\n                    idx=np.random.permutation(KK.shape[0])\n                    idx=idx[:200]\n                    KK=KK[np.ix_(idx,idx)]\n                #Wi, LW, LWi, W_logdet = pdinv(KK)\n                #sign,W_logdet2=np.linalg.slogdet(KK)\n                chol  = spla.cholesky(KK, lower=True)\n                W_logdet=np.sum(np.log(np.diag(chol)))\n                # Uses the identity that log det A = log prod diag chol A = sum log diag chol A\n    \n                #second_term=-0.5*W_logdet2\n                second_term=-W_logdet\n            except: # singular\n                return -np.inf\n            \n\n            logmarginal=first_term+second_term-0.5*len(myY)*np.log(2*3.14)\n                \n            if np.isnan(np.asscalar(logmarginal))==True:\n                print(\"lengthscale_x={:f} lengthscale_t={:f} first term ={:.4f} second  term ={:.4f}\".format(\n                        lengthscale,lengthscale_t,np.asscalar(first_term),np.asscalar(second_term)))\n\n            #print(lengthscale, lengthscale_t,midpoint,growth,\"logmarginal:\",logmarginal)\n            return np.asscalar(logmarginal)\n        \n        logmarginal=0\n\n        if not isinstance(hyper,list) and len(hyper.shape)==2:\n            logmarginal=[0]*hyper.shape[0]\n            growth=hyper[:,3]\n            midpoint=hyper[:,2]\n            lengthscale_t=hyper[:,1]\n            lengthscale_x=hyper[:,0]\n            for idx in range(hyper.shape[0]):\n                logmarginal[idx]=compute_log_marginal_with_logistic_hyper(lengthscale_x[idx],\\\n                           lengthscale_t[idx],midpoint[idx],growth[idx],noise_delta)\n        else:\n            lengthscale_x,lengthscale_t,midpoint,growth=hyper\n            logmarginal=compute_log_marginal_with_logistic_hyper(lengthscale_x,lengthscale_t,\\\n                                                                 midpoint,growth,noise_delta)\n        return logmarginal\n\n#    def optimize_lengthscale_SE_maximizing(self,previous_theta,noise_delta):\n#        \"\"\"\n#        Optimize to select the optimal lengthscale parameter\n#        \"\"\"\n#      Optimize to select the optimal lengthscale parameter\n        \"\"\"\n        \n        # define a bound on the lengthscale\n        SearchSpace_l_min=0.03\n        SearchSpace_l_max=0.3\n        \n        SearchSpace_midpoint_min=-2\n        SearchSpace_midpoint_max=3\n        \n        SearchSpace_growth_min=0.5\n        SearchSpace_growth_max=2\n        #mySearchSpace=[np.asarray([SearchSpace_lengthscale_min,SearchSpace_lengthscale_max]).T]\n        \n [SearchSpace_midpoint_min,SearchSpace_midpoint_max],[SearchSpace_growth_min,SearchSpace_growth_max]])\n        \n        lengthscale_tries = np.random.uniform(mySearchSpace[:, 0], mySearchSpace[:, 1],size=(20, 4))\n\n        # evaluate\n        self.flagOptimizeHyperFirst=0 # for efficiency\n\n        logmarginal_tries=self.log_marginal_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(lengthscale_tries,noise_delta)\n\n        #find x optimal for init\n        idx_max=np.argmax(logmarginal_tries)\n        lengthscale_init_max=lengthscale_tries[idx_max]\n        #print lengthscale_init_max\n        \n        myopts ={'maxiter':30*self.dim,'maxfun':30*self.dim}\n\n        x_max=[]\n        max_log_marginal=None\n        \n        res = minimize(lambda x: -self.log_marginal_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(x,noise_delta),lengthscale_init_max,\n     val=self.log_marginal_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(res.x,noise_delta)  \n        \n        # Store it if better than previous minimum(maximum).\n        if max_log_marginal is None or val >= max_log_marginal:\n            if 'x' not in res:\n                x_max = res\n            else:\n                x_max = res.x\n            max_log_marginal = val\n            #print res.x\n\n        return x_max\n\n\n#    def optimize_lengthscale(self,previous_theta_x, previous_theta_t,noise_delta):\n#\n#        prev_theta=[previous_theta_x,previous_theta_t]\n# optimize_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(self,prev_hyper,noise_delta):\n        # optimize both GP lengthscale and logistic hyperparameter\n\n            \n        #prev_theta=[prev_theta_x,prev_theta_t,prev_midpoint,prev_growth]\n        newlengthscale,newlengthscale_t,newmidpoint,newgrowth=self.optimize_lengthscale_SE_logistic_hyper(prev_hyper,noise_delta)\n        self.hyper['lengthscale_x']=newlengthscale\n        self.hyper['lengthscale_t']=self.hyper['lengthscale_t']\n        \n        # refit the model\n        temp=np.hstack((self.X,self.T))\n        ur = unique_rows(temp)\n\n        # update Y here\n        Y_original=transform_logistic(self.Y_curves,newmidpoint,newgrowth,self.SearchSpace[-1,1])\n        Y=(Y_original-np.mean(Y_original))/np.std(Y_original)\n        self.Y=Y\n        #\n        self.fit(self.X[ur],self.T[ur],self.Y[ur],self.Y_curves)\n        \n        return newlengthscale,newlengthscale_t,newmidpoint,newgrowth\n\n\n    def compute_var(self,X,T,xTest,tTest):\n        \"\"\"\n        compute variance given X and xTest\n        \n        Input Parameters\n        ----------\n        X: the observed points\n        xTest: the testing points \n        \n        Returns\n        -------\n        diag(var)\n        \"\"\" \n        \n        xTest=np.asarray(xTest)\n        xTest=np.atleast_2d(xTest)\n        \n        tTest=np.asarray(tTest)\n        tTest=np.atleast_2d(tTest)\n        tTest=np.reshape(tTest,(-1,1))\n        \n        if self.kernel_name=='SE':\n            #Euc_dist=euclidean_distances(xTest,xTest)\n            #KK_xTest_xTest=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x'])+np.eye(xTest.shape[0])*self.noise_delta\n            #ur = unique_rows(X)\n            myX=X\n            myT=T\n            \n            Euc_dist_x=euclidean_distances(myX,myX)\n            #exp_dist_x=np.exp(-np.square(self.Euc_dist_x)/lengthscale)+np.eye(len(myX))*noise_delta\n        \n            Euc_dist_t=euclidean_distances(myT,myT)\n            #exp_dist_t=np.exp(-np.square(self.Euc_dist_t)/lengthscale_t)+np.eye(len(myX))*noise_delta      \n        \n            KK=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']-np.square(Euc_dist_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])\n                +np.eye(len(myX))*self.noise_delta\n                    \n                 \n            Euc_dist_test_train_x=euclidean_distances(xTest,X)\n            #Exp_dist_test_train_x=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x'])\n            \n            Euc_dist_test_train_t=euclidean_distances(tTest,T)\n            #Exp_dist_test_train_t=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])\n            \n            KK_xTest_xTrain=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])\n                \n        try:\n            temp=np.linalg.solve(KK,KK_xTest_xTrain.T)\n        except:\n            temp=np.linalg.lstsq(KK,KK_xTest_xTrain.T, rcond=-1)\n            temp=temp[0]\n            \n        #var=KK_xTest_xTest-np.dot(temp.T,KK_xTest_xTrain.T)\n        var=np.eye(xTest.shape[0])-np.dot(temp.T,KK_xTest_xTrain.T)\n        var=np.diag(var)\n        var.flags['WRITEABLE']=True\n        var[var<1e-100]=0\n        return var \n\n    \n        \n    def predict(self,xTest, eval_MSE=True):\n        \"\"\"\n        compute predictive mean and variance\n        Input Parameters\n        ----------\n        xTest: the testing points \n        \n        Returns\n        -------\n        mean, var\n        \"\"\"    \n\n        if len(xTest.shape)==1: # 1d\n            xTest=xTest.reshape((-1,self.X.shape[1]+1))\n            \n        tTest=xTest[:,-1]\n        tTest=np.atleast_2d(tTest)\n        tTest=np.reshape(tTest,(xTest.shape[0],-1))\n        \n        xTest=xTest[:,:-1]\n        \n        # prevent singular matrix\n        temp=np.hstack((self.X,self.T))\n        ur = unique_rows(temp)\n        \n        X=self.X[ur]\n        T=self.T[ur]\n                \n        Euc_dist_x=euclidean_distances(xTest,xTest)\n        Euc_dist_t=euclidean_distances(tTest,tTest)\n\n        KK_xTest_xTest=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']-np.square(Euc_dist_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])\n            +np.eye(xTest.shape[0])*self.noise_delta\n        \n        Euc_dist_test_train_x=euclidean_distances(xTest,X)\n        \n        Euc_dist_test_train_t=euclidean_distances(tTest,T)\n        \n        KK_xTest_xTrain=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_x)/self.hyper['lengthscale_x']-np.square(Euc_dist_test_train_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])\n            \n        #Exp_dist_test_train_x*Exp_dist_test_train_t\n  \n        # using Cholesky update\n        mean=np.dot(KK_xTest_xTrain,self.alpha)\n        v=np.linalg.solve(self.L,KK_xTest_xTrain.T)\n        var=KK_xTest_xTest-np.dot(v.T,v)\n        \n\n        return mean.ravel(),np.diag(var)  \n\n    def posterior(self,x):\n        # compute mean function and covariance function\n        return self.predict(self,x)\n        \n    \n\n# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-\n\"\"\"\nCreated on Tue Mar 29 11:49:58 2016\n\n\"\"\"\n\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom bayes_opt.acquisition_functions import  \"\"\"\n        Main optimization method.\n\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        x: recommented point for evaluation\n        \"\"\"\n \n        # init a new Gaussian np.asscalar(self.T_original[-1]),np.asscalar(self.Y_original[-1]), np.asscalar(self.Y_original.max())))\n",
    "Experiment Result": "### Gaussian Process (GP) Model\n- **Kernel:** Product kernel, defined as `k(x,x')*k(t,t')` where both `k` are RBF (Squared Exponential) kernels.\n- **Noise:** Initial `noise_delta = 5e-4` (default in `ProductGaussianProcess`). `noise_upperbound = 1e-2`.\n- **Hyperparameter Optimization:** Lengthscales (`lengthscale_x`, `lengthscale_t`) for the RBF kernels and parameters of the Logistic preference function (`midpoint`, `growth`) are optimized. The optimization objective is to maximize the GP's log marginal likelihood.\n- **Optimization Method:** L-BFGS-B (from `scipy.optimize.minimize`).\n- **Initial GP Hyperparameters:** `lengthscale_x = 0.02`, `lengthscale_t = 0.2` (if not provided).\n- **Initial Logistic Hyperparameters:** `midpoint = 0.0`, `growth = 1.0` (if not provided).\n- **GP/Logistic Hyperparameter Optimization Frequency:** Every `2 * self.dim` iterations (where `self.dim` is the dimensionality of the search space).\n- **Optimization Attempts:** Multiple random starts (`20` in `optimize_lengthscale_SE_logistic_hyper`).\n- **Optimization Iterations (L-BFGS-B):** `maxiter = 30 * self.dim`, `maxfun = 30 * self.dim` for hyperparameter optimization.\n- **Condition Number Threshold:** Log condition number of the GP covariance matrix is kept below `15` (`self.threshold_cond = 15`) during data augmentation to prevent ill-conditioning.\n\n### Training Curve Compression\n- **Method:** Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function, specifically `apply_one_transform_logistic` and `transform_logistic` from `bayes_opt/curve_compression.py`.\n- **Parameters:** `midpoint` (m0) and `growth` (g0) of the Logistic function are learned dynamically as part of the GP hyperparameter optimization.\n\n### Training Time Cost Model\n- **Approximation:** Linear regressor (`sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression`).\n- **Fitting:** The linear regressor is fitted to the observed `(X, T)` pairs and their corresponding `Y_cost_original` values at each `suggest_nextpoint` iteration.\n\n### Acquisition Function\n- **Type:** Modified Expected Improvement, specifically `ei_mu_max` (default `acq_name`). This variant uses the maximum of the GP's predictive mean (`mu_max`) as the incumbent value for EI calculation.\n- **Balancing Utility and Cost:** The acquisition function `utility_cost_evaluation` combines utility (e.g., `_ei` output) and predicted cost (`mean_cost` from linear regressor) as `np.log(utility) - np.log(mean_cost)`. The optimization goal is to minimize the negative of this value.\n- **Maximization (Minimization of Negative):** Multi-start L-BFGS-B optimization is used.\n    - **Random Starts for `acq_max_scipy` (used by `acq_max`):** `1 * dim` random starts.\n    - **Random Starts for `acq_min_scipy_kwargs` (used by `acq_utility_cost`):** `3 * dim` random starts.\n    - **Optimization Iterations (L-BFGS-B):** `maxiter = 10 * dim`, `maxfun = 20 * dim` for acquisition function maximization.\n\n### Selective Data Augmentation\n- **Selection Criterion:** Points are added based on maximizing GP predictive uncertainty (`_pure_exploration` acquisition function) at the selected hyperparameter `x`, over a range of `t` (training iterations).\n- **Adaptive Selection:** Augmentation points are adaptively chosen to keep the logarithm of the condition number of the covariance matrix below a threshold (`self.threshold_cond = 15`). Augmentation stops if uncertainty is too low (`pred_var_value < (self.gp.noise_delta + 1e-3)`) or if `t` values repeat.\n- **Maximum Augmentations:** `max_n_augmentation = 10` virtual observations per real observation (calculated as `np.int(t_max * self.max_n_augmentation)`).\n- **Cost for Augmented Points:** The cost for virtual observations is interpolated linearly from the real observation's cost (`y_cost_estimate = y_cost_original * virtual_obs_t`).\n\n### Initialization\n- **Number of Initial Points:** `n_init_points = 3` (default in `init` method).\n- **Initial Point Generation:** Random uniform sampling within the search space bounds. The `t` dimension for initial points is fixed to `MaxIter` (upper bound of the iteration space).\n- **Random Seed:** Set at the beginning of `init` method for reproducibility."
}{
    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces \"Partitioned Neural Networks,\" a novel, efficient, and scalable method for hyperparameter optimization. This method is inspired by marginal likelihood, operates in a single training run without requiring a separate validation set, and is computationally cheaper than previous marginal likelihood-based approaches. It achieves this by partitioning both the training data and the neural network model into K shards and parameter partitions, respectively. An \"out-of-training-sample\" loss is then defined for hyperparameter optimization. The method can optimize various hyperparameters, including neural architecture, data augmentation strategies, regularization, and dropout rates, across different tasks. It is particularly beneficial in federated learning settings, where it helps overcome practical challenges and reduces communication overhead.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves approximating the marginal likelihood objective (LML) through a partitioning scheme. The neural network's weights are divided into C partitions (w1, ..., wC), and the training dataset is split into C non-overlapping chunks (D1, ..., DC). Subnetworks w(k)s are constructed by using the first k weight partitions (w1, ..., wk) and setting the remaining parameters to default values (e.g., initialization). During training, each weight partition wk is optimized on data from D1:k using its corresponding subnetwork w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are optimized using the \"out-of-training-sample\" loss, which is the sum of losses of subnetworks w(k-1)s on the data chunk Dk (data unseen by w(k-1)s). This process is performed in a single training run with interleaved stochastic gradient updates for both model parameters and hyperparameters. The primary partitioning scheme employed in experiments is random weight partitioning, where a fixed proportion of weights in each layer is randomly assigned to a partition. The LML objective is proven to be a lower-bound on the marginal likelihood under mild assumptions.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on various tasks, including a toy input selection task with a synthetic dataset (30 features, 15 informative) using MLPs; learning invariances through data augmentation on MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants (rotCIFAR10, rotTinyImagenet, RotMNIST) using CNNs, fixupResNets (8, 14), and ResNet-50; optimizing a feature extractor on the full CIFAR10 dataset with a Wide ResNet-20; and federated learning settings with non-i.i.d. splits of MNIST, RotMNIST, CIFAR10, and RotCIFAR10 across 100 clients, using a convolutional network (MNIST) and ResNet-9 (CIFAR10). Baselines included traditional validation set optimization, Post-hoc Diagonal Laplace, Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, Last-layer Marginal Likelihood, and FedAvg (with/without Augerino). Performance was evaluated using test accuracy and log-likelihood. Hyperparameters optimized included input masks, affine image augmentations (rotation, shear, translation, scale), entire feature extractors, and dropout rates. Training parameters involved Adam or SGD optimizers, specific batch sizes (e.g., 256 for input selection, 200 for MNIST, 250 for CIFAR10), learning rates, and non-uniform data/parameter partitioning (e.g., 8 uniform chunks, or 3 chunks with [80%,10%,10%] data split). For data augmentation tasks, 20 augmentation samples were typically used.",
    "Limitations": "The method inherently requires an additional forward-backward pass to update hyperparameters, leading to increased computational costs, though these are generally less than existing alternative methods. Furthermore, partitioned networks empirically often require more training iterations to achieve convergence. The partitioning of the network can constrain its capacity, potentially resulting in a slight performance loss compared to a full, non-partitioned network that has been optimally tuned. The method also introduces an additional hyperparameter: the partitioning strategy itself (e.g., number of chunks and the relative proportions of data and parameters assigned to each chunk), which may require tuning for optimal performance on specific tasks.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could involve exploring the dynamic partitioning of network parameters during training, as opposed to fixed partitioning. Investigations into alternative partitioning schemes are suggested to potentially reduce the overhead associated with separate forward-backward passes for hyperparameters. Research into alleviating the performance loss caused by inherent capacity constraints of partitioned networks, possibly by adjusting training rounds or increasing network capacity, is also proposed. Further exploration of different hyperparameter gradient accumulation strategies or update scheduling intervals is noted. Lastly, different sequential updating schemes for partitions within the federated learning context could be a promising area of research.",

    "Title": "Implicit differentiation of Lasso-type models for hyperparameter optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces an efficient implicit differentiation algorithm, called Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation, for hyperparameter optimization of non-smooth Lasso-type models. This method leverages the sparsity of solutions and the fixed-point iterations of Block Coordinate Descent (BCD) to compute hypergradients without solving potentially ill-conditioned linear systems. It decouples the computation of regression coefficients from the Jacobian, leading to faster and more stable hyperparameter optimization. The proposed method is shown to converge linearly to the true Jacobian once the support is identified and empirically outperforms a range of standard hyperparameter optimization techniques, including other gradient-based and non-gradient-based methods, across various high-dimensional datasets and criteria (held-out loss and SURE).",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology relies on implicit differentiation tailored for non-smooth Lasso-type problems by exploiting the fixed-point equations inherent in proximal BCD algorithms. Unlike classical implicit differentiation, which inverts a Hessian matrix, the proposed method (Algorithm 2) works in two steps: first, it solves the inner optimization problem to compute the regression coefficients (ˆβ) and identify their support (ˆS). Second, it applies forward differentiation recursion steps, restricted only to the active support, to compute the Jacobian (ˆJ) of ˆβ with respect to the hyperparameters. This approach avoids explicitly forming or inverting large, potentially ill-conditioned dense matrices. Theoretical propositions demonstrate that this implicit forward differentiation converges linearly to the true Jacobian for Lasso and weighted Lasso models once the support is identified. The method can be used with any Lasso solver and is applied within a bi-level optimization framework for hyperparameter selection via gradient descent.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experiments compare the proposed Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation (Algorithm 2) against implicit differentiation (Algorithm 1), forward iterative differentiation (Algorithm 3), backward iterative differentiation (Algorithm 4), grid-search, random-search, lattice hypercube sampling, and Bayesian optimization. Evaluation was performed on: 1. Held-out loss for Lasso (single hyperparameter) and MCP (two hyperparameters) models, using real-world datasets: rcv1 (n=20,242, p=19,959), 20news (n=11,314, p=130,107), and finance (n=16,087, p=1,668,737). Data was split into training, validation, and test sets. 2. Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE) for Lasso (single hyperparameter) and weighted Lasso (p hyperparameters), using synthetic data with n=100 and p varying from 200 to 10,000, sparse true coefficients, and Gaussian noise (SNR=3). Metrics included objective function values (e.g., validation loss, objective minus optimum), test set loss, relative Mean Squared Error (MSE) of coefficients, and total computation time. A vanilla Block Coordinate Descent (Algorithm 5) was used as the inner solver for all methods, with a stopping tolerance of 10^-5. Hypergradient methods employed a line-search strategy with warm-started initializations.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical guarantees for the convergence of the Jacobian (Proposition 2) rely on the assumption of a unique solution for the inner Lasso-type optimization problem. Although cases of non-unique solutions are often pathological and rare, the theory does not strictly apply in such settings, though empirical results suggest the algorithms can still be used. The developed theory explicitly does not cover non-convex regularization penalties, such as the MCP, though numerical experiments indicated proper behavior in these cases. Classical implicit differentiation (Algorithm 1) can suffer from slow convergence and numerical instability when dealing with ill-conditioned linear systems, particularly in high-dimensional settings with large support sizes. Backward iterative differentiation was found to be computationally slow and memory-intensive for the problem types studied, making it less practical.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include extending the theoretical framework to rigorously cover non-convex regularization penalties beyond the empirically tested MCP. Further exploration of cases where the inner Lasso-type optimization problem might have non-unique solutions is suggested, potentially leading to new gradient-based or non-gradient-based methods. The two-step nature of the proposed implicit forward differentiation algorithm allows for its integration with advanced state-of-the-art Lasso solvers that employ techniques like active sets or screening rules. This integration could be further explored, especially given that such advanced solvers can introduce discontinuities with respect to hyperparameters, posing challenges for single-step automatic differentiation approaches.",

with L1 prior as regularizer (aka the Lasso).\n\n    The optimization objective for Lasso is:\n    (1 / (2 * n_samples)) * ||y - Xw||^2_2 + alpha * ||w||_1\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    estimator: sklearn estimator\n        Estimator used to solve the optimization problem. Must follow the\n        scikit-learn API.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, estimator=None):\n        self.estimator = estimator\n\n    def _init_dbeta_ddual_var(self, X, y, mask0=None, jac0=None,\n                              dense0=None, compute_jac=True):\n        n_samples, n_features = X.shape\n        dbeta = np.zeros(n_features)\n        if jac0 is None or not compute_jac:\n            ddual_var = np.zeros(n_samples)\n        else:\n            dbeta[mask0] = jac0.copy()\n            ddual_var = - X[:, mask0] @ jac0.copy()\n        return dbeta, ddual_var\n\n    def _init_beta_dual_var(self, X, y, mask0=None, dense0=None):\n        beta = np.zeros(X.shape[1])\n        if dense0 is None or len(dense0) == 0:\n            dual_var = y.copy()\n            dual_var = dual_var.astype(np.float)\n        else:\n            beta[mask0] = dense0.copy()\n            dual_var = y - X[:, mask0] @ dense0\n        return beta, dual_var\n\n    @staticmethod\n    @njit\n    def _update_beta_jac_bcd(\n            X, y, beta, dbeta, dual_var, ddual_var,\n            alpha, L, compute_jac=True):\n        n_samples, n_features = X.shape\n        non_zeros = np.where(L != 0)[0]\n\n        for j in non_zeros:\n            beta_old = beta[j]\n            if compute_jac:\n                dbeta_old = dbeta[j]\n                # compute derivatives\n            zj = beta[j] + dual_var @ X[:, j] / (L[j] * n_samples)\n            beta[j] = ST(zj, alpha[j] / L[j])\n            # beta[j:j+1] = ST(zj, alpha[j] / L[j])\n            if compute_jac:\n                dzj = dbeta[j] + X[:, j] @ ddual_var / (L[j] * n_samples)\n                dbeta[j:j+1] = np.abs(np.sign(beta[j])) * dzj\n                dbeta[j:j+1] -= alpha[j] * np.sign(beta[j]) / L[j]\n                # update residuals\n                ddual_var -= X[:, j] * (dbeta[j] - dbeta_old)\n            dual_var -= X[:, j] * (beta[j] - beta_old)\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def _get_jac(dbeta, mask):\n        return dbeta[mask]\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def get_L(X):\n        \"\"\"Compute Lipschitz constant of datafit.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        X: array-like, shape (n_samples, n_features)\n            Design matrix.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        L: float\n            The Lipschitz constant.\n        \"\"\"\n        if issparse(X):\n            return slinalg.norm(X, axis=0) ** 2 / (X.shape[0])\n        else:\n            return norm(X, axis=0) ** 2 / (X.shape[0])",
    "Experiment Result": "The method relies on implicit differentiation for Lasso-type problems, using fixed-point equations of proximal BCD for inner optimization and forward differentiation recursion for Jacobian computation, restricted to the active support. It operates within a bi-level optimization framework for hyperparameter selection via gradient descent.\n\n**Algorithms/Methods Compared:**\n-   **First-order methods:** ImplicitForward (the core method), Forward, Implicit, Backward. Variants like 'implicit_forward_approx' (ImplicitForward with geometric tolerance decrease) and 'sota' (ImplicitForward with Lightning.classification.LinearSVC or celer).     "Title": "A New Linear Scaling Rule for Private Adaptive Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the significant challenge of hyperparameter optimization (HPO) in differentially private (DP) deep learning, where existing methods often neglect the privacy cost of HPO or suffer from high computational expense. The core contribution is an adaptive HPO method based on a 'new linear scaling rule' that uses low-cost, small-budget trials to estimate optimal hyperparameters and then scales them up to the full privacy budget. This method is shown to reduce the computation and privacy cost of HPO by an order of magnitude without compromising performance. It achieves state-of-the-art results across 22 benchmark tasks in computer vision and natural language processing, including pretraining and finetuning, and various architectures, across a wide range of privacy budgets (ε ∈ [0.01, 8.0]), all while explicitly accounting for the privacy cost of HPO. The method outperforms random search, grid search (when privacy cost is considered), and several prior private HPO approaches, and demonstrates robustness even when there's a large shift between public and private data.",
    "Methodology": "The proposed adaptive private HPO method, termed the 'new linear scaling rule,' centers on identifying and optimizing a single scalar hyperparameter, `r = η × T`, which represents the product of the learning rate (η) and the number of training iterations (T). This simplification is motivated by theoretical analysis linking the excess empirical risk of private Gradient Descent (GD) to a 'noisy radius,' which is shown to be proportional to `η × T`. The method estimates the optimal `r` for a given privacy budget `ε` by sampling `r(ε)` at two distinct, small privacy budgets (e.g., `ε1 = 0.1, ε2 = 0.2`) using random search (cheap trials). A linear function (`r = mε + b`) is then fitted to these two points to approximate the relationship between `r` and `ε`, allowing for the estimation of `r` for any target `εf`. The estimated `r*` is subsequently decomposed back into `η` and `T`. Key design choices for private training include using full-batch gradient descent (DP-GD) to maximize the signal-to-noise ratio of updates, unit clipping (C=1) for per-sample gradients to avoid optimization bias, zero initialization for linear classifier weights, and integrating momentum (ρ=0.9) to accelerate convergence. The privacy accounting uses the Privacy Loss Variable (PLV) method (based on f-DP/Gaussian DP), which offers tighter guarantees than RDP, particularly for smaller `ε` values, and explicitly accounts for the privacy cost incurred during HPO.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was extensively evaluated on 22 benchmark tasks. Image classification tasks included ImageNet (finetuning), CIFAR10 (training from scratch and finetuning), CIFAR100, FashionMNIST, STL10, and EMNIST. For distribution shift robustness, datasets such as CIFAR10 → STL, CIFAR10p1, CIFAR10C, CIFAR100 → CIFAR100C, Waterbirds, FMoW, and Camelyon17 were used. Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks comprised SQuAD (Question Answering), text classification from the GLUE benchmark (SST-2, QNLI, QQP, MNLI(m/mm)), and next-word generation tasks using PersonaChat, WikiText-2, and Enron Emails. Models included various vision Transformers (beit, beitv2, ViT-base/large) and convolutional architectures (convnext), often fine-tuning only a linear layer on extracted features. For NLP, GPT-2 and RoBERTa-base were fine-tuned, and OPT-13B with DP-SGD-LoRA was tested on SQuADv2. Hyperparameters `η` and `T` were primarily optimized across logarithmic grids for `η` and epoch ranges for `T`. Privacy budgets (ε) ranged from 0.01 to 8.0. Validation involved comparisons against random search, grid search (without HPO privacy cost), and prior RDP-based private HPO methods, as well as parameter-free optimizers and other DP transfer learning approaches. Ablation studies investigated the impact of architectural choices, initialization, batch size, optimizer type, privacy accounting method, clipping norm, momentum, weight averaging, and data augmentation. Performance was measured by accuracy for classification tasks and perplexity for language modeling. The work provides code for reproducibility.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical convergence analysis relies on assumptions (strong convexity, smoothness, Lipschitzness, bounded gradients) that do not universally hold for complex neural network training, though empirical results demonstrate practical success. The adaptive HPO method has a higher runtime and less parallelizability compared to purely random or embarrassingly parallel grid search, as it involves sequential steps. The paper also notes that while a linear approximation for `r(ε)` works well, higher-order approximations might be better in some cases but would incur higher privacy costs for HPO. Moreover, decomposing `r` into more hyperparameters (e.g., `η, T, B, C`) was empirically shown to degrade performance. For very small `ε` on challenging datasets like ImageNet, the marginal utility of HPO trials might become negligible, as trials with extremely small budgets may not provide sufficient information for effective scaling. Additionally, traditional weight averaging techniques (EMA, SWA) were found to be ineffective in the proposed optimization regime, where the model's trajectory continuously moves away from initialization.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could extend the theoretical analysis to incorporate more complex factors like momentum acceleration and clipping bias, or generalize it to a broader range of neural network architectures. Investigating RDP analysis for their adaptive method is another potential avenue. Applying their HPO framework to privatize other existing non-private HPO methods or to alternate threat models involving public data in pretraining is suggested. Exploring the integration of their HPO to tune parameters of parameter-free DP optimizers like DPAdamWOSM, and scaling ideas from other private HPO works (e.g., subsampling-based methods) to larger datasets like ImageNet, could also be fruitful. Further research into fitting higher-degree polynomials for `r(ε)` to improve approximation, while balancing the increased privacy cost, is mentioned. Optimizing a joint product of more hyperparameters (e.g., `η, T, B, C`) more effectively, and improving the parallelization strategies of the adaptive HPO method to reduce wall-clock time, are practical extensions. Finally, further fine-tuning of language models with longer iterations, especially for larger epsilon values, could improve utility.",
    "Experiment Code": "The implementation directly related to the differentially private training mechanism is primarily located within the `private_transformers` library and integrated into the `examples/classification` training loop. The key components are:\n\n1.  **PrivacyEngine Initialization and Attachment (examples/classification/run_classification.py)**\n    The `PrivacyEngine` orchestrates the DP-SGD process. It is initialized with critical privacy and training parameters and then `attach`ed to the optimizer, overriding its `step`, `zero_grad`, and `virtual_step` methods to incorporate DP-SGD logic.\n    ```python\n    # ... inside main() function\n    if privacy_args.non_private:\n        privacy_args.noise_multiplier = 0.\n        privacy_args.per_example_max_grad_norm = None\n    else:\n        total_train_batch_size = training_args.gradient_accumulation_steps * training_args.per_device_train_batch_size\n        privacy_engine = PrivacyEngine(\n            module=model,\n            batch_size=total_train_batch_size,\n            sample_size=len(train_dataset),\n            epochs=training_args.num_train_epochs,\n            max_grad_norm=privacy_args.per_example_max_grad_norm,\n            noise_multiplier=privacy_args.noise_multiplier,\n            target_epsilon=privacy_args.target_epsilon,\n            target_delta=privacy_args.target_delta,\n            accounting_mode=privacy_args.accounting_mode,\n            clipping_mode=privacy_args.clipping_mode,\n            skip_checks=True,\n        )\n        # Originally, it could have been null.\n        privacy_args.noise_multiplier = privacy_engine.noise_multiplier\n        privacy_args.target_delta = privacy_engine.target_delta\n\n        print('privacy_args: ')\n        print(json.dumps(privacy_args.__dict__, indent=4))\n        privacy_engine.attach(optimizer)\n    ```\n\n2.  **DP-SGD Step Execution (private_transformers/privacy_engine.py)**\n    The `PrivacyEngine`'s `step` and `virtual_step` methods, specifically `_ghost_step` (for `clipping_mode=\"ghost\"`), perform the core DP-SGD operations:\n    -   **Per-example gradient clipping**: In `_ghost_step`, a double-backward pass (`_double_backward`) is invoked. The first backward computes initial gradients, and the second backward, using `coef_sample` (clipping factor derived from `max_grad_norm` and per-example gradient norms), applies the clipping. The per-example gradient norms are accumulated in `param.norm_sample` during the first backward pass via hooks, and clipping factors (`coef_sample`) are computed as `max_grad_norm / (norm_sample + numerical_stability_constant)`.\n    -   **Gradient accumulation**: Clipped per-example gradients are accumulated into `param.summed_grad` across micro-batches.\n    -   **Noise addition**: After accumulation, `_create_noisy_clipped_gradient` adds Gaussian noise to the `param.summed_grad` (which becomes `param.grad`) before the optimizer's original `step` function is called. The standard deviation of the noise is `noise_multiplier * max_grad_norm`.\n\n    ```python\n    # Excerpt from private_transformers/privacy_engine.py\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _create_noisy_clipped_gradient(self):\n        signals, noises = [], []\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            # ... (assertions for param.summed_grad omitted for brevity) ...\n            param.grad = param.summed_grad # Overrides the .grad attribute\n\n            if self.record_snr:\n                signals.append(param.grad.reshape(-1).norm(2))\n\n            if self.noise_multiplier > 0 and self.max_grad_norm > 0:\n                noise = torch.normal(\n                    mean=0,\n                    std=self.noise_multiplier * self.max_grad_norm,\n                    size=param.size(),\n                    device=param.device,\n                    dtype=param.dtype,\n                )\n                param.grad += noise\n                # ... (noise tracking omitted) ...\n                del noise\n\n            param.grad /= self.batch_size # Scale by inverse batch size\n        # ... (SNR calculation and engine lock omitted) ...\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _ghost_step(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        # ... (lock check omitted) ...\n        self._ghost_virtual_step(loss)\n        self._create_noisy_clipped_gradient()\n\n    @torch.no_grad()\n    def _ghost_virtual_step(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        self._double_backward(loss) # Performs double backward for clipping\n\n        for name, param in self.named_params:\n            if hasattr(param, 'summed_grad'):\n                param.summed_grad += param.grad\n            else:\n                param.summed_grad = param.grad\n            # ... (gradient cleanup omitted) ...\n\n    @torch.enable_grad()\n    def _double_backward(self, loss: torch.Tensor):\n        first_loss = loss.sum()\n        first_loss.backward(retain_graph=True)\n\n        autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.ghost_grad)\n        # ... (clear previous grad omitted) ...\n        coef_sample = self.get_coef_sample() # Calculate clipping factor\n        second_loss = (coef_sample * loss).sum(dim=0)\n        second_loss.backward()\n\n        autograd_grad_sample.set_hooks_mode(BackwardHookMode.ghost_norm) # Reset hook mode for next forward pass\n\n    def get_coef_sample(self) -> torch.Tensor:\n        norm_sample = self.get_norm_sample()\n        return torch.clamp_max(self.max_grad_norm / (norm_sample + self.numerical_stability_constant), 1.)\n\n    def get_norm_sample(self) -> torch.Tensor:\n        # Collects per-example gradient norms using `param.norm_sample` populated by hooks\n        norm_sample = torch.stack([param.norm_sample for name, param in self.named_params], dim=0).norm(2, dim=0)\n        return norm_sample\n    ```\n\n3.  **Per-Sample Gradient Hooks (private_transformers/autograd_grad_sample.py and private_transformers/supported_layers_grad_samplers.py)**\n    The `add_hooks` function registers forward and backward hooks to modules. The forward hooks (`_capture_activations`) save activations, while backward hooks (`_capture_backprops`) trigger `_compute_grad_sample`. Specialized functions like `_compute_linear_grad_sample` and `_compute_embedding_grad_sample` in `supported_layers_grad_samplers.py` compute per-sample gradients and their norms (`param.norm_sample`) for various layer types, essential for the ghost clipping mechanism. This is implicitly called when `loss.backward()` is triggered within `_double_backward`.",
    "Experiment Result": "The adaptive private HPO method, termed the 'new linear scaling rule,' involves an HPO search phase (sampling `r(ε)` at small privacy budgets, fitting a linear function) which is not explicitly implemented in the provided code. The code implements the private training mechanism given a target `r` (learning rate × number of training iterations).\n\nHere are the experimental settings extracted from the `examples/classification` directory (default values for private training):\n\n*   **Optimizer**: `torch.optim.AdamW` with default betas (momentum `β1=0.9`, `β2=0.999`).\n*   **Learning Rate (η)**: `5e-4` (default for non-private is `5e-5`). A linear decay schedule is applied (`lr_decay=yes`).\n*   **Number of Training Iterations (T)**: Set by `num_train_epochs`, which is dynamically scaled based on the task. For 'sst-2', `num_train_epochs` is `3` epochs; for 'qnli' `6` epochs; for 'qqp' and 'mnli' `18` epochs (computed as `base_num_train_epochs * factor` where `base_num_train_epochs=3` and `factor` depends on the task).\n*   **Gradient Clipping**: `per_example_max_grad_norm=0.1` (which corresponds to `C=0.1` in the `PrivacyEngine`). `clipping_mode=\"ghost\"` is used, implementing ghost clipping for efficient per-sample gradient clipping.\n*   **Batch Size**: `per_device_train_batch_size=20`. The effective batch size for one DP update (`PrivacyEngine.batch_size`) is calculated as `gradient_accumulation_steps * per_device_train_batch_size`. `gradient_accumulation_steps` are derived such that the effective batch size is approximately `base_batch_size = 1000 * factor` (e.g., `1000` for 'sst-2', `2000` for 'qnli', `6000` for 'qqp' and 'mnli').\n*   **Privacy Budget (ε)**: `target_epsilon=8` (default). The method describes sampling at `ε1=0.1, ε2=0.2` for HPO, with `target_epsilon` being the final chosen privacy budget.\n*   **Privacy Delta (δ)**: Not explicitly set via command-line arguments, thus defaults to `sample_size ** -1.1` within the `PrivacyEngine`.\n*   **Privacy Accounting**: `accounting_mode=\"rdp\"` (default in `src/compiled_args.py`). Note: The method description mentions the \"Privacy Loss Variable (PLV) method (based on f-DP/Gaussian DP)\" for tighter guarantees, which corresponds to `accounting_mode=\"glw\"` or `\"all\"` within `private_transformers`, but the default setting is RDP.\n*   **Model Initialization**: The model is loaded from a pre-trained checkpoint (e.g., `roberta-base`). The method mentions \"zero initialization for linear classifier weights,\" which would typically apply to newly added classifier layers or if `model.init_weights()` re-initializes existing ones. The prompt-tuning models (e.g., `RobertaForPromptFinetuning`) call `self.init_weights()` from `BertPreTrainedModel` upon instantiation after loading pre-trained weights."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO addresses the core challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (BO) of adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model. FastBO efficiently offers strong performance and provides a general strategy to extend any single-fidelity optimization method to a multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO adaptively determines the fidelity for each configuration using two key concepts: the \"efficient point\" and the \"saturation point.\" The efficient point `ei` for a configuration `λi` is the resource level `r` where doubling resources yields performance improvement below a small threshold `δ1`, signifying an optimal resource-to-performance balance for surrogate model fitting. The saturation point `si` is the resource level `r` beyond which performance variations fall below a small threshold `δ2`, indicating performance stabilization. The FastBO process involves a warm-up stage for early observations, learning curve estimation, adaptive extraction of efficient and saturation points, evaluating configurations up to their efficient points to update the surrogate model, and a post-processing stage where promising configurations are further evaluated up to their saturation points to identify the optimal configuration. This adaptive fidelity identification strategy is highlighted as a general approach to extend single-fidelity methods to the multi-fidelity setting.",

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "This research addresses the scalability challenges of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions, where incorporating gradient information into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogates leads to computationally expensive kernel matrices (O(n^3d^3) for inversion, O(n^2d^2) for multiplication). The main contributions are: 1) Deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for gradient kernel matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiply. 2) Proposing an Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for transformations and algebraic combinations of a wide range of kernels (e.g., neural network, RBF-network, spectral mixture kernels). 3) Deriving analogous structures for kernel matrices arising from Hessian observations, reducing their complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2) for matrix-vector multiplication. These advancements allow FOBO to scale to higher dimensions while maintaining modeling flexibility.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology revolves around exploiting the inherent structure of kernel matrices when derivative information is incorporated. For a large class of kernels (isotropic, stationary linear functional, dot product), the authors show that the G operator (∇x∇⊤y) applied to the kernel k(x,y) results in highly structured and data-sparse blocks G[k](xi,xj). A chain rule is applied for kernels of the form k = f ◦ g, showing that G[f ◦ g] is a rank-one correction to G[g]. Rules for sums and products of kernels are derived, including direct sums and products leading to O(d) complexity. Vertical rescaling and warping transformations are also analyzed, demonstrating how their gradient kernel matrices can be factorized or represented as low-rank corrections. This systematic application of rules forms a structure-aware AD algorithm. For Hessian observations, analogous structured representations are derived for isotropic kernels, leading to an O(n^2d^2) complexity. The approach is implemented in CovarianceFunctions.jl, a Julia package that uses user-defined types to represent computational graphs of kernel functions and leverages ForwardDiff.jl for general derivatives, with lazy evaluation for O(1) memory complexity.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The methods were evaluated through several numerical experiments: 1) Scaling on Synthetic Data: Benchmarks were conducted to study the practical scaling of matrix-vector multiplications with gradient and Hessian kernel matrices using rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. Experiments varied dimensions (d) and number of observations (n) and were run on single-thread and 24-thread setups. 2) Comparison to Prior Work: Performance was compared against popular GP libraries (GPyTorch, GPFlow, Scikit-Learn, DragonFly, BayesOpt), highlighting their lack of efficient gradient-based inference. A specific comparison was made with D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018), an approximate method, in terms of execution time and accuracy for RBF gradient kernel matrices. 3) Bayesian Optimization: The proposed methods were used within a BO framework, employing an expected improvement acquisition function. Optimization algorithms (random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with restarts, BO, FOBO) were benchmarked on non-convex test functions: Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin, in 4 and 16 dimensions. A specialized 'quadratic mixture kernel' was also tested for BO and FOBO.",
    "Limitations": "The global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and thus the proposed methods, while improving BO performance, cannot generally solve this problem. For instance, the optimum of the 16D Ackley function was elusive for all tested methods, likely due to its shrinking domain of attraction in higher dimensions. The research primarily focused on first-order information (gradients), with Hessian observations demonstrated for computational scaling and feasibility but not explicitly integrated into BO experiments. A comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO is left for future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) Linking the proposed structure-aware AD technology with Maclaurin et al. (2015)'s algorithm for computing hyper-parameter gradients, enabling efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters. 2) Developing analysis-based fast algorithms, such as Fast Kernel Transforms, for gradient kernels to further address the O(n^2) complexity that can still be expensive for very large numbers of observations (n). 3) Exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, particularly when converging to a minimum, using sparse linear solvers. 4) Exploring the utility of Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, given their established benefits for integration (e.g., Laplace approximation).",

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability limitations of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high-dimensional spaces, where incorporating gradient information into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate models leads to computationally expensive kernel matrices (O(n^2d^2) for matrix-vector multiplication, O(n^3d^3) for inversion). The main contribution is observing that a wide range of kernels gives rise to structured matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiply for gradient observations and O(n^2d^2) for Hessian observations. A programmatic approach leveraging this structure for transformations and combinations of kernels, which constitutes a structure-aware automatic differentiation (AD) algorithm, is proposed. This method applies to virtually all canonical kernels and automatically extends to complex kernels like neural network, radial basis function network, and spectral mixture kernels without additional derivations, thus scaling FOBO to high dimensions and enabling flexible, problem-dependent modeling. Numerical experiments demonstrate improved scaling and applicability, comparisons against existing techniques, and a specific application to Bayesian Optimization.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting the block-data-sparse structure of kernel matrices that arise when conditioning Gaussian Processes on gradient and Hessian information. The approach is based on deriving analytical structured representations for the matrix-valued operator G = ∇x∇⊤y acting on various kernel functions. For kernels of the form k(x,y) = f(proto(x,y)) (where proto can be isotropic, stationary linear functional, or dot product), G[proto] is simple. A chain rule is derived, showing G[f ◦g] is a rank-one correction to G[g]. Rules for sums and products of kernels are developed, showing how complexity is maintained (O(dr) for r products). Direct sums and products for separable kernels yield O(d) complexity. Vertical rescaling and input warping (k(x,y) = h(u(x),u(y))) are also shown to maintain efficient, structured representations (O(n^2d)). Analogous structures are derived for Hessian kernel matrices, reducing the matrix-vector multiplication complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2). An Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework, implemented in Julia as CovarianceFunctions.jl, programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for general kernel transformations and combinations. This implementation uses lazy evaluation to achieve O(1) memory complexity for matrix-vector multiplies and leverages ForwardDiff.jl for fallback derivative computations. For Bayesian Optimization experiments, a composite kernel f = g+h ∼ GP(0,k(x,y) + (x·y + c)^2) was used, combining a quadratic component (g) with a Matérn-5/2 kernel (h) to model objective functions, exploiting the structured AD.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes three main parts: 1) Scaling on Synthetic Data: Benchmarks of matrix-vector multiplications (MVMs) with gradient and Hessian kernel matrices were conducted using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. The scaling was analyzed with respect to the number of observations (n) and dimensionality (d), with experiments run on a single thread for scaling plots and 24 threads on 12 cores for heatmaps to demonstrate parallel architecture applicability. 2) Comparison to Prior Work: The proposed methods were compared against popular GP libraries (GPyTorch, GPFlow, Scikit-Learn) and an approximate method called D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018), specifically focusing on MVM complexity and real-world performance for RBF gradient kernel matrices. Accuracy comparisons were also performed for D-SKIP against the proposed exact method. 3) Bayesian Optimization: Various optimization algorithms were benchmarked on Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin test functions (Bingham and Surjanovic, 2013). Input domains were scaled to [-1,1]^d and outputs to [0,1], with global optima shifted. Strategies included random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with random restarts (L-BFGS-R), standard BO, BO with a quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO, and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants used the expected improvement acquisition function, numerically optimized with L-BFGS. Optimality gaps were averaged over 128 independent experiments for 4D and 16D problems. A restart mechanism for the acquisition function optimization was implemented, replacing points too close to previous observations with random points.",
    "Limitations": "The research acknowledges that global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and the proposed methods, while improving BO performance, cannot generally solve this problem. For instance, the optimum of the 16D Ackley function remained elusive for all tested methods, likely due to its domain of attraction shrinking exponentially with dimensionality. Although structured representations for Hessian observations were derived and their improved computational scaling was demonstrated, they were not utilized for Bayesian Optimization experiments in this work, with the focus primarily remaining on first-order information. A comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO is left for future work. Additionally, while the methods achieve linear-in-d MVM complexity, the O(n^2) complexity with respect to the number of observations (n) can still become expensive for very large datasets.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) Linking the proposed structured Automatic Differentiation technology with Maclaurin et al. (2015)'s algorithm for computing hyper-parameter gradients, enabling efficient First-Order Bayesian Optimization of hyper-parameters. 2) Addressing the O(n^2) computational complexity for large numbers of observations by exploring analysis-based fast algorithms for gradient kernels, similar to Ryan et al. (2022)'s Fast Kernel Transform, particularly in low dimensions. This also involves exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, especially when nearing a minimum, using sparse linear solvers. 3) Conducting a more comprehensive comparison of first and second-order Bayesian Optimization methods, given the theoretical advances and practical infrastructure provided by this work. 4) Investigating the utility of Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, as the benefits of second-order information for integration are well-established (e.g., Laplace approximation).",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Efficient Nonmyopic Bayesian Optimization via One-Shot Multi-Step Trees",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper provides the first efficient implementation of general multi-step lookahead Bayesian optimization (BO), formulated as a sequence of nested optimization problems within a multi-step scenario tree. It introduces a novel 'one-shot' approach to jointly optimize all decision variables in the full tree, combined with an efficient method for multi-step Gaussian process 'fantasization'. This allows multi-step expected improvement to be computationally tractable and demonstrates superior performance over existing myopic and nonmyopic methods on various benchmarks, achieving significant optimization improvements while maintaining competitive wall times.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves approximating the intractable multi-step BO problem using a differentiable, tree-based acquisition function. Instead of nested optimization, it proposes a 'one-shot' joint optimization of all decision variables in a multi-step scenario tree by leveraging the reparameterization trick, making the objective fully differentiable for gradient-based optimization. This is coupled with 'fast-fantasies,' an efficient linear algebra method for updating GPyTorch’s LOVE caches for posterior inference in each tree step, and a highly parallelizable implementation on accelerated hardware. Special cases explored include 'Multi-Step Path' (single path in subtrees, linear growth) and 'Non-Adaptive Approximation' (ENO), which approximates future decisions with a one-step batch value function. Optimization is performed using L-BFGS-B with random restarting and an advanced warm-starting strategy, utilizing Gauss-Hermite quadrature for samples.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The methods were evaluated on nine 'hard' synthetic functions and seven real hyperparameter tuning functions (SVM, LDA, logistic regression, neural network tuning on Boston Housing and Breast Cancer, active learning of robot pushing). Each experiment started with 2d random initial observations and performed 20d BO iterations, repeated 100 times per function and method. A Gaussian Process (GP) with a constant mean and a Matérn 5/2 ARD kernel was used as the surrogate model, with hyperparameters re-estimated after each iteration. Performance was measured using GAP = (yi − y0)/(y∗ − y0). Experiments were run on CPU Linux machines, each using one core, although GPU speedups for fantasization were noted separately. Baselines included Expected Improvement (EI), BINOCULARS (12.EI.s variant), and the 'envelope two-step' (ETS) method.",
    "Limitations": "The general one-shot optimization problem's dimension grows exponentially with the lookahead horizon (k), practically limiting it to relatively small horizons (less than 5). The paper notes diminishing returns with increasing lookahead horizon beyond 3-step, suggesting potential issues with optimizing increasingly complex objectives or increased reliance on model accuracy. The 'envelope two-step' (ETS) method, a baseline, relies on the potentially unrealistic assumption that maximizers of second-stage value functions are global optima, which can lead to objective discontinuities and problematic differentiation. The broader impact statement also acknowledges that Bayesian optimization, as a field, does not inherently preclude optimizing nefarious or ethically complicated objectives.",
    "Future Research Directions": "An interesting future endeavor is to investigate the application of the developed framework to other problems, such as Bayesian quadrature.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null
}{
    "Title": "Accelerating Look-ahead in Bayesian Optimization: Multilevel Monte Carlo is All you Need",
    "Main Contributions": "The primary contribution is the introduction and application of Multilevel Monte Carlo (MLMC) to multi-step look-ahead Bayesian optimization (BO) methods, which traditionally suffer from exponentially increasing computational complexity due to nested expectations and maximizations. The paper demonstrates that MLMC can achieve the canonical Monte Carlo convergence rate (O(ε^-2) or O(ε^-2(log ε)^2)) for these problems, independently of dimension and without smoothness assumptions, compared to the suboptimal O(ε^-2(k+1)) rate of naive Monte Carlo for k nested operations. This significantly reduces the computational cost of approximating acquisition functions, making multi-step look-ahead BO more efficient and practical.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology leverages MLMC to approximate analytically intractable multi-step look-ahead acquisition functions formulated as Markov Decision Processes (MDPs). It uses a nested Sample Average Approximation (SAA) to handle nested optimization and Monte Carlo (MC) estimations. MLMC constructs a telescoping sum of estimators from low to high accuracy, performing most simulations with low-accuracy and low-cost to reduce overall computational complexity. An antithetic coupling approach is introduced to further improve the convergence rate. The approach is applied to approximate the maximizer of the acquisition function directly, as well as the acquisition function itself (for theoretical analysis), within a Gaussian Process-based BO framework.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method's benefits were verified numerically on several benchmark examples from BoTorch. These include a 1D toy example and higher-dimensional synthetic functions such as Ackley (d=2), DropWave (d=2), Branin (d=2), Hartmann6 (d=6), Cosine8 (d=8), Griewank, Goldstein-Price, and 6-hump Camel. Performance was measured using Normalized Mean Squared Error (NMSE) and GAP metrics, tracking the convergence of the BO algorithm with respect to cumulative wall time. Experiments involved 20 to 200 realizations, an initial BO run starting with 2 × d observations, and the Matérn kernel for the Gaussian Process. MLMC sample sizes and levels were tuned based on theoretical formulas related to a target accuracy (ε). L-BFGS with multi-start initialization was used for optimization, employing a 'point matching' strategy to align maximizers across MLMC levels.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical analysis for the multilevel estimator of the maximizer (x*ML) relies on strong assumptions (Assumptions 1, 2, and 3), particularly Assumption 3 regarding the convergence rate of maximizer increments, which remains to be formally proven. MLMC performance requires careful tuning of levels and sample numbers to balance variance and bias effectively. The benefits of MLMC are more significant at higher accuracy requirements, implying less gain at very low accuracy. The current framework mainly focuses on two- and three-step look-ahead, and MLMC may not achieve the canonical rate for an increasing number of look-ahead steps due to the curse of dimensionality and potential violation of regularity assumptions. Furthermore, the theoretically easier-to-analyze multilevel value function estimator showed worse practical performance in BO compared to the direct optimizer estimator.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include further theoretical analysis to provide a complete and subtle theory for the multilevel maximizer estimator, especially regarding unbiased increments. Extensions to more than three look-ahead steps are planned, potentially using Multi-index Monte Carlo (MIMC) to address the curse of dimensionality under mixed regularity assumptions. Investigating randomized MLMC and MIMC is proposed to eliminate bias and reduce costly tuning. Exploring the integration of multilevel quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) or multi-index QMC could further reduce computational complexity to O(ε^-1) for smooth integrands, although performance may degenerate for non-smooth functions. Applying the MLMC approach to constrained Bayesian optimization and exploring other acquisition functions, such as the logEI family, are also suggested.",

  return new_candidate, new_value\n\n\nclass CustomqMultiStepLookahead(qMultiStepLookahead):\n    r\"\"\"Inherited from qMultiStepLookahead allowing passing inner samplers\"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n collapse_fantasy_base_samples: bool = True\n    ) -> None:\n        r\"\"\"rewrite the initializer of qMultistepLookahead by allowing passing inner samplers\"\"\"\n\n        if objective is not None and not isinstance(objective, MCAcquisitionObjective):\n            raise UnsupportedError(\n                \"`qMultiStepLookahead` got a non-MC `objective`. This is not supported.\"\n                \" Use `posterior_transform` and `objective=None` instead.\"\n            )\n\n        super(MCAcquisitionFunction, self).__init__(model=model)\n        self.batch_sizes = batch_sizes\n        if not ((num_fantasies is None) ^ (samplers is None)):\n            raise UnsupportedError(\n                \"qMultiStepLookahead requires exactly one of `num_fantasies` or \"\n                \"`samplers` as arguments.\"\n            )\n        if samplers is None:\n            # If collapse_fantasy_base_samples is False, the `batch_range_override`\n            # is set on the samplers during the forward call.\n            samplers: List[MCSampler] = [\n                SobolQMCNormalSampler(sample_shape=torch.Size([nf]))\n                for nf in num_fantasies\n            ]\n        else:\n            num_fantasies = [sampler.sample_shape[0] for sampler in samplers]\n        self.num_fantasies = num_fantasies\n        #
