Can be\n                a `batch_shape`-shaped tensor, which in case of a batched model\n                specifies potentially different values for each element of the batch.\n            sampler: The sampler used to draw base samples. Defaults to\n                `SobolQMCNormalSampler(num_samples=512, collapse_batch_dims=True)`\n            objective: The MCAcquisitionObjective under which the samples are evaluated.\n                Defaults to `IdentityMCObjective()`.\n            posterior_transform: A PosteriorTransform (optional).\n            X_pending:  A `m x d`-dim Tensor of `m` design points that have been\n                submitted for function evaluation but have not yet been evaluated.\n                Concatenated into X upon forward call. Copied and set to have no\n                gradient.\n        \"\"\"\n\n        super().__init__(\n            model=model,\n            best_f=best_f,\n            sampler=sampler,\n            objective=objective,\n            posterior_transform=posterior_transform,\n            X_pending=X_pending,\n        )\n\n    @concatenate_pending_points\n    @t_batch_mode_transform()\n    def forward(self, X: Tensor) -> Tensor:\n        r\"\"\"Evaluate qExpectedImprovement antithetic on the candidate set `X`.\n\n        Args:\n            X: A `batch_shape x q x d`-dim Tensor of t-batches with `q` `d`-dim design\n                points each.\n\n        Returns:\n            A `batch_shape'`-dim Tensor of antithetic of Expected Improvement values at the given\n            design points `X`, where `batch_shape'` is the broadcasted batch shape of\n            model and input `X`.\n        \"\"\"\n        posterior = self.model.posterior(\n            X=X, posterior_transform=self.posterior_transform\n        )\n        samples = self.sampler(posterior)\n\n        obj_1 = self.objective(samples[::2], X=X)\n        obj_1 = (obj_1 - self.best_f.unsqueeze(-1).to(obj_1)).clamp_min(0)\n        q_ei_1 = obj_1.max(dim=-1)[0].mean(dim=0)\n\n        obj_2 = self.objective(samples[1::2], X=X)\n        obj_2 = (obj_2 - self.best_f.unsqueeze(-1).to(obj_2)).clamp_min(0)\n        q_ei_2 = obj_2.max(dim=-1)[0].mean(dim=0)\n\n        inds_1 = torch.argmax(q_ei_1, dim=-1)\n        inds_2 = torch.argmax(q_ei_2, dim=-1)\n\n        for i in range(len(inds_1)):\n            temp = q_ei_2[i, inds_1[i]].item()\n            q_ei_2[i, inds_1[i]] = q_ei_2[i, inds_2[i]]\n            q_ei_2[i, inds_2[i]] = temp\n\n        return (q_ei_1 + q_ei_2)/2\n\n    def _sample_forward(self, obj: Tensor) -> Tensor:\n        r\"\"\"Evaluate qExpectedImprovement per sample on the candidate set `X`.\n\n        Args:\n            obj: A        )\n        else:\n            twoqEI =ThreeStepIncAntEI(\n                model=self.model,\n                batch_sizes=self.batch_sizes,\n                num_fantasies=None,\n distance matrix of two vectors\n    xsq = torch.sum(x ** 2, dim=(1, 2))  # (N,)\n    ysq = torch.sum(y ** 2, dim=(1, 2))  # (M,)\n\n    mixprod = -2 * x.view(x.shape[0], -1) @ y.view(y.shape[0], -1).T alpha=1, beta=1.5, gamma=1, meanc=1, varc=1, var0=1, match_mode='point'):\n    r\"\"\"Optimize acquisition functions using mlmc.\n\n    Args:\n        inc_function: define the acquisition function np.arange(dl, L + 1)\n\n    Ncon = (np.sum(np.sqrt(varc * 2 ** (-levels * (beta - gamma)))) + np.sqrt(var0 * 2 ** gamma)) / (eps ** 2)\n    # number of outer samples at each level\n    Nl = np.ceil(np.maximum(Ncon * np.sqrt(varc * 2 ** (-levels * (beta + gamma))), 2)).astype(int)\n\n    # TODO: Check one-shot ml f is the same as single f(z^*)\n    # z is the optimizer and f is the corresponding optimum\n    f, z = 0., 0.\n    # matching optimizer such that the optimizers at levels correspond\n    match = None\n    if match_mode == 'backward':\n        for level, n in zip(reversed(levels - dl), reversed(Nl)):\n            zl, fl, match = inc_function.sample_candidate(level, dl, n, match=match, match_mode=match_mode)\n            if level == (levels - dl)[-1]:\n                f = fl\n                z = zl\n            else:\n                f += fl\n                z += zl\n            opt_ind = torch.argmax(f, dim=0)\n        return z[opt_ind], f[opt_ind], Nl\n    else:\n        for level, n in zip(levels - dl, Nl):\n            zl, fl, match = inc_function.sample_candidate(level, dl, n, match=match, match_mode=match_mode)\n            if match_mode == 'point':\n                f += fl\n                z += zl\n            else:\n                if level == (levels - dl)[0]:\n                    f = fl\n                    z = zl\n     "Experiment Result": "The MLMC approach is implemented for approximating multi-step look-ahead acquisition functions, specifically two-step (`qEIMLMCTwoStep`) and three-step (`qEIMLMCThreeStep`) Expected Improvement (EI). It utilizes an antithetic coupling (`qExpectedImprovementAnt`, `ThreeStepIncAntEI`) to enhance convergence. The core MLMC optimization is handled by `optimize_mlmc` and `optimize_mlmc_three` functions.\n\n### Experimental Settings:\n\n*   **Objective Functions:** Experiments are typically conducted on synthetic test functions like `Ackley(dim=2, negate=True)` or `Hartmann(dim=6, negate=True)`. The `SelfDefinedFunction`, `Goldstein`, `Ackley5`, `Levy10`, `SixHumpCamel2`, `Cosine`, and `Branin2` functions are also available.\n*   **Initial Observations:** Number of initial observations is `2 * dim`, where `dim` is the dimensionality of the objective function.\n*   **BO Iterations:** `n_runs` is set to 30 for Bayesian Optimization (BO) runs.\n*   **Realisations:** `R` is 20 for multiple independent runs to compute average performance and error bars.\n*   **Optimizer Settings (LBFGS):**\n    *   `num_restarts`: 20-30 restarts (e.g., 20 in `runBO` examples, 30 in `tutorials/tests.py` for MC, 10 for MLMC three-step).\n    *   `raw_samples`: 100-256 raw samples for each restart (e.g., 256 in `runBO` examples, 100 for MC/MLMC two-step in `tutorials/tests.py`, 256 for MLMC three-step).\n*   **MLMC Parameters:**\n    *   `eps` (desired accuracy): Ranges from 0.1 to 0.25 (e.g., 0.2 for `testTwoEI`, 0.15 for `testWholeBO`, 1e-1 or 0.1 for specific `tutorials/tests.py` examples).\n    *   `dl` (actual starting level): Typically set to 3.\n    *   `alpha`: Bias rate, default 1.\n    *   `beta`: Variance rate, default 1.5.\n    *   `gamma`: Cost rate, default 1.\n    *   `meanc`: Bias constant, default 1.\n    *   `varc`: Variance constant for increment, default 1.\n    *   `var0`: Variance for single level estimator, default 1.\n    *   `match_mode`: 'point', 'forward', or 'backward'. 'point' is a common setting.\n*   **Acquisition Function Settings:**\n    *   `q` (batch size for optimization): For two-step MLMC, `q=[1, 2]` (meaning 1-batch, 2-step lookahead). For three-step MLMC, `q=[1, 1, 1]` (meaning 1-batch, 3-step lookahead).\n    *   `num_samples` (outer MC samples for MC baseline): `np.round((1 / np.power(eps, 2))).astype(int)`.\n    *   `inner_mc_samples` (inner MC samples for MC baseline): Typically the same as `num_samples` for the two-step acquisition.\n*   **Model:** Gaussian Process (GP) with `FixedNoiseGP` or `SingleTaskGP` (with `Normalize` input and `Standardize` outcome transforms for `SingleTaskGP`). Fitted using `ExactMarginalLogLikelihood`.\n*   **Data Types and Seeds:**\n    *   `torch.set_default_dtype(torch.double)`.\n    *   `torch.manual_seed(66)` for overall experiments, or `torch.manual_seed(0)` for specific optimization calls in `tutorials/tests.py`.\n    *   `device = torch.device(\"cuda\" if torch.cuda.is_available() else \"cpu\")`.\n*   **Performance Metrics:** Normalized Mean Squared Error (NMSE) of the best observed value against the true optimal value, averaged over realisations, and plotted against expected cumulative wall time."

    "Title": "A General Framework for User-Guided Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces ColaBO, a novel Bayesian-principled framework that enables practitioners to inject arbitrary prior beliefs about function properties (such as the likely location of the optimizer, the optimal value, or preference relations) into Bayesian Optimization (BO). Unlike traditional BO methods that are limited to kernel structure modifications, ColaBO allows for a more flexible and direct incorporation of user knowledge. The framework is generally applicable across different Monte Carlo acquisition functions and types of user beliefs. Empirical results demonstrate that ColaBO substantially accelerates optimization when accurate prior information is provided and maintains approximately default performance even when the prior is misleading, showcasing its robustness.",
    "Methodology": "ColaBO redefines the Gaussian Process (GP) prior by integrating an additional, user-defined prior belief over function properties, denoted as ρ(f). This user belief acts as a likelihood, weighting draws from the GP prior to form a belief-weighted prior p(f|ρ) ~ p(f)ρ(f). When observations (D) are obtained, the posterior is updated as p(f|D, ρ) ~ ρ(f)p(f|D). The framework leverages a pathwise update mechanism based on decoupled posterior sampling, where the user belief ρ(f) influences the initial draw from the prior, while observed data D affects a deterministic update. Since the belief-weighted prior and posterior are generally non-Gaussian, ColaBO relies on Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition functions (like Expected Improvement and Max-Value Entropy Search) which are modified to incorporate the ρ(f) weighting through rejection sampling on function draws from the prior, followed by pathwise updates based on observed data.",
    "Experimental Setup": "ColaBO's performance was evaluated on two categories of tasks: synthetic functions with known optima and real-world hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks. Synthetic benchmarks included Hartmann (3D, 4D, 6D), Levy (5D), Rosenbrock (6D), and Stybtang (7D) functions, with varying noise levels and search spaces. Priors for these tasks were meticulously constructed to be either 'well-located' (10% offset from optimum) or 'poorly located' (maximally offset). HPO tasks were sourced from LCBench (5 deep learning tasks, 6D) and PD1 (3 deep learning tasks, 4D) benchmarking suites, utilizing existing priors from MF-Prior-Bench. ColaBO variants (ColaBO-LogEI and ColaBO-MES) were benchmarked against vanilla LogEI, vanilla MES, πBO, and decoupled Thompson Sampling. All methods used a squared exponential kernel (with some Matérn-5/2 kernel experiments in the appendix), MAP hyperparameter estimation, and were initialized with the prior's mode and 2 Sobol samples (except HPO tasks with 1 initial sample). Approximation quality was also assessed by comparing the argmax of ColaBO-LogEI to vanilla LogEI on Hartmann (3D). Batch evaluations (q=3) were demonstrated using a sequential greedy technique.",
    "Limitations": "ColaBO's reliance on multiple Monte Carlo steps leads to significant computational expense, potentially requiring tens of seconds per evaluation to achieve desired accuracy. The process of obtaining draws from the prior over the optimum (ρ*x) scales exponentially with the dimensionality of the prior, which could render ColaBO impractical for very high-dimensional user priors. The Random Fourier Features (RFF) approximation used introduces a bias, particularly pronounced with the Matérn 5/2-kernel, making ColaBO best suited for the squared exponential kernel. Additionally, the framework cannot effectively utilize Quasi-MC for draws from the predictive posterior (only for RFF weights) and cannot fix base samples for acquisition function consistency across the search space.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on developing more accurate and computationally efficient sampling procedures for the belief-weighted prior. Exploring variational inference or pre-trained model approaches could lead to a belief-biased model with an analytical posterior, significantly reducing ColaBO's runtime and broadening its applicability. Another promising direction is applying ColaBO to multi-fidelity optimization, which could further enhance its efficiency, particularly for expensive deep learning pipelines.",
    "Experiment Code": "def get_botorch(experiment: Experiment, data: Data, search_space: Optional[SearchSpace] = None, dtype: torch.dtype = torch.double, device: torch.device = DEFAULT_TORCH_DEVICE, transforms: List[Type[Transform]] = Cont_X_trans + Y_trans, transform_configs: Optional[Dict[str, TConfig]] = None, model_constructor: TModelConstructor = get_and_fit_model, model_predictor: TModelPredictor = predict_from_model, acqf_constructor: TAcqfConstructor = get_NEI,  acqf_optimizer: TOptimizer = scipy_optimizer, refit_on_cv: bool = False, refit_on_update: bool = True, optimization_config: Optional[OptimizationConfig] = None) -> TorchModelBridge:\n    \"\"\"Instantiates a BotorchModel.\"\"\"\n    if data.df.empty:  #     "Experiment Result": "The ColaBO method, which extends Gaussian Process (GP) Bayesian Optimization with user-defined prior beliefs, is not explicitly implemented in the provided repository content. However, the repository provides implementations for standard GP models and Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition functions that ColaBO modifies.     "Title": "Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization via Deep Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Deep Neural Network Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (DNN-MFBO) to address the limitations of existing multi-fidelity BO methods that either ignore or over-simplify the strong, complex, and potentially nonlinear/nonstationary correlations across fidelities. The main contributions include developing a deep neural network-based multi-fidelity surrogate model that flexibly captures these complicated relationships to improve objective function estimation. It also introduces a computationally tractable and highly efficient mutual information-based acquisition function, calculated using sequential, fidelity-wise Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment-matching. The method demonstrates superior optimization performance, achieving better regrets with smaller query costs in synthetic benchmarks and real-world engineering design applications, alongside significant speedups in acquisition function calculation compared to state-of-the-art multi-output Gaussian Process-based methods.",
    "Methodology": "DNN-MFBO models each fidelity using a dedicated neural network (NN). For fidelities 'm > 1', the input to the NN `xm` is concatenated from the original input `x` and the output from the previous fidelity `fm-1(x)`. For the first fidelity (`m=1`), the input is `x`. The NN output is `fm(x) = w_m^T * phi_theta_m(xm)`, where `w_m` (output layer weights) are treated as random variables with a standard normal prior, and `theta_m` (other NN weights) are hyper-parameters. A stochastic variational learning algorithm is employed to jointly estimate the multivariate Gaussian posterior `q(w_m) = N(w_m | mu_m, Sigma_m)` and the hyper-parameters by maximizing an Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) using the reparameterization trick. The acquisition function is defined as `a(x, m) = (1/lambda_m) * I(f*, fm(x)|D)`, a mutual information-based criterion for maximum entropy search, where `lambda_m` is the query cost for fidelity `m`. Due to nonlinear coupling, `p(fm(x)|D)` is approximated as a Gaussian using fidelity-wise moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature. The conditional entropy term `Ep(f*|D)[H(fm(x)|f*, D)]` is approximated via Monte-Carlo sampling of the function maximum `f*` and further approximating `H(fm(x)|f*, D)` with `H(fm(x)|fM(x) <= f*, D)`. This approximation for conditional entropy also uses moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature (or truncated Gaussian for the highest fidelity). Acquisition function optimization uses L-BFGS with random initialization, with gradients computed via automatic differentiation (TensorFlow).",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method was evaluated on three synthetic benchmark functions: Branin (2D input, 3 fidelities), Park1 (4D input, 2 fidelities), and Levy (2D input, 3 fidelities). Additionally, two real-world engineering design applications were used: Mechanical Plate Vibration Design (3 material properties, 2 fidelities for mesh density) and Thermal Conductor Design (3 shape parameters for a hole, 2 fidelities for mesh density). Performance metrics included Simple Regret (SR), Inference Regret (IR) for synthetic tasks, and the average best queried function value for real-world tasks, alongside average query time. DNN-MFBO was compared against MF-SKO, MF-GP-UCB, MF-PES, MF-MES, MTNN-BO, and SF-MES. DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO were implemented with TensorFlow using ReLU activation. Other baselines used their original Matlab/Python/Numpy implementations. Hyperparameter tuning for DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO (NN depth [2,12], width [32,512], learning rate [10e-5, 10e-1]) involved initial searches with SMAC3 and subsequent manual tuning. ADAM was used for stochastic training over 5,000 epochs. Acquisition function optimization for DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO used L-BFGS with random initialization, while some competitors used global optimization (DIRECT/grid search) before L-BFGS. Initial training data consisted of random queries (e.g., Branin/Levy: 20, 20, 2 samples for fidelities 1, 2, 3; Park1 and real-world: 20, 5 samples for lower and higher fidelities). Query costs (`lambda_m`) were set (e.g., (1, 10, 100) for 3-fidelity tasks and (1, 10) for 2-fidelity tasks). All experiments were repeated five times on a Linux workstation (16-core Intel Xeon E5-2670 CPU, 16GB RAM), and average results with standard error bars were reported.",
    "Limitations": "The current work explicitly focuses on Bayesian optimization with **discrete fidelities**, suggesting that its applicability or efficiency for continuous fidelities is not directly addressed. The use of L-BFGS with random initialization for maximizing the acquisition function, while effective, might be considered less globally robust compared to methods that incorporate an initial global optimization search (e.g., DIRECT or grid search) as used by some baseline methods. Furthermore, the calculation of the conditional entropy term `H(fm(x)|fM(x) <= f*, D)` serves as a 'reasonable approximation' to `H(fm(x)|fM(x) = f*, D)`, indicating that this part of the acquisition function is not exactly computed. The variational inference framework itself provides an approximation to the true posterior distributions, and for tractability, only the output layer weights of the neural networks are treated as random variables, while other NN weights are fixed as hyper-parameters, which is a simplification of a fully Bayesian treatment.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A clear direction for future research involves extending the DNN-MFBO framework to handle **continuous fidelities**, as the current work explicitly concentrates on discrete fidelities. Further investigation could also explore more advanced or globally robust optimization techniques for the acquisition function beyond L-BFGS with random initialization, potentially to enhance convergence guarantees, even though the current approach already shows strong empirical performance. Additionally, while the model flexibly captures complex correlations, deeper exploration into alternative uncertainty quantification techniques or more elaborate neural network architectures within the multi-fidelity context could yield further improvements.",
    "Experiment Code": "# File Path: test_functions/python_related/push_world.py\n#!/usr/bin/env python\n# Author: Ari Anders and Zi Wang\nfrom Box2D import *\nfrom Box2D.b2 import *\nimport numpy as     "Experiment Result": "The repository content describes a Box2D physics simulation environment that serves as a testbed for machine learning methods. The experimental setup involves simulating robotic pushing tasks, where the objective is typically to minimize the Euclidean distance between objects' final positions and target goal positions. Key parameters and settings of this simulation environment are detailed below:\n\n**Common Simulation Settings (from `push_world.py` and `generate_simudata*.py` defaults):**\n- **Physics Engine:** Box2D\n- **GUI:** Disabled by default (`b2WorldInterface(False)`).\n- **World Gravity:** (0.0, 0.0)\n- **Simulation Frame Rate (`TARGET_FPS`):** 100 FPS\n- **Time Step (`TIME_STEP`):** 0.01 seconds (1/TARGET_FPS)\n- **Velocity/Position Iterations (`VEL_ITERS`, `POS_ITERS`):** 10, 10\n- **Default Object Shape (`oshape`):** 'circle'\n- **Default Object Size (`osize`):** 1 unit\n- **Object Friction (`ofriction`):** 0.01\n- **Object Density (`odensity`):** 0.05\n- **Base Friction (`bfriction`):** 0.01\n- **Hand/Robot End-effector Shape (`hand_shape`):** 'rectangle'\n- **Hand/Robot End-effector Size (`hand_size`):** Typically (0.3,1) for single-robot scenarios or (1,0.3) for multi-robot scenarios, when `hand_shape` is 'rectangle'.\n- **Robot Force Unit (`forceunit`):** 30.0 for 'rectangle' hands, 100.0 for 'circle' hands.\n- **Friction Joint (between base and object/hand):** MaxForce=5, MaxTorque=2 (for object); MaxForce=2, MaxTorque=2 (for hand).\n- **Random Noise in Robot Velocity:** Added with `np.random.normal(0,0.1)` or `np.random.normal(0,0.01)` to `rvel` and `rvel2`.\n\n**Specific Experiment Configurations (from `generate_simudata*.py` scripts):**\n\n1.  **`generate_simudata3.py` (Single Robot, Single Object, Fixed Direction Push):**\n    - **Scenario:** One robot pushing a circular object.\n    - **Inputs:** `rx`, `ry` (robot initial position), `gx`, `gy` (goal position), `sys.argv[3]` (base for `simu_steps`).\n    - **`simu_steps`:** Integer value, `int(float(sys.argv[3]) * 10)`.\n    - **Robot Initial Angle (`init_angle`):** Calculated as `np.arctan(ry/rx)`.\n    - **Push Direction:** Fixed, pointing from the robot's initial location towards the object's initial location, scaled to a magnitude of 5.\n    - **Objective:** Minimize `np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx, gy]) - ret)` where `ret` is the final object position.\n\n2.  **`generate_simudata4.py` (Single Robot, Single Object, Controlled Angle Push):**\n    - **Scenario:** One robot pushing a circular object.\n    - **Inputs:** `rx`, `ry` (robot initial position), `gx`, `gy` (goal position), `init_angle` (robot's initial angle), `sys.argv[3]` (base for `simu_steps`).\n    - **`simu_steps`:** Integer value, `int(float(sys.argv[3]) * 10)`.\n    - **Robot Velocity:** Direction derived from `(-rx, -ry)`, normalized, and scaled by 10 (`xvel = xvel / regu * 10`).\n    - **Objective:** Minimize `np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx, gy]) - ret)`.\n\n3.  **`generate_simudata_2robot2thing.py` (Two Robots, Two Objects):**\n    - **Scenario:** Two robots pushing two distinct objects.\n    - **Object 1:** Rectangle of size (0.5,0.5), initial position (0,2).\n    - **Object 2:** Circle of size 1, initial position (0,-2).\n    - **Robot End-effector Size:** (1,0.3).\n    - **Inputs (Robot 1):** `rx`, `ry` (position), `xvel`, `yvel` (velocity components), `simu_steps` (duration), `init_angle` (initial angle), `rtor` (angular velocity).\n    - **Inputs (Robot 2):** `rx2`, `ry2` (position), `xvel2`, `yvel2` (velocity components), `simu_steps2` (duration), `init_angle2` (initial angle), `rtor2` (angular velocity).\n    - **Goal 1:** `gx`, `gy` for Object 1.\n    - **Goal 2:** `gx2`, `gy2` for Object 2.\n    - **Objective:** Minimize the sum of Euclidean distances to respective goals: `np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx, gy]) - ret1) + np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx2, gy2]) - ret2)`."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO addresses the challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization (BO) for Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS) of adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each configuration to fit the surrogate model. It introduces the novel concepts of 'efficient point' and 'saturation point' for each configuration, enabling adaptive fidelity identification and efficient high-quality performance. The proposed adaptive fidelity identification strategy is also shown to generalize, allowing any single-fidelity method to be extended to a multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO extends Bayesian Optimization (BO) by introducing two key concepts: the efficient point (e_i) and the saturation point (s_i). The efficient point for a configuration lambda_i is defined as the minimum resource level 'r' where doubling the resources yields performance improvement below a small threshold delta_1, serving as the appropriate fidelity for fitting the surrogate model due to its optimal resource-to-performance balance. The saturation point is defined as the minimum resource level 'r' beyond which the observed performance no longer exhibits notable variations, characterizing the fidelity where performance stabilizes. The FastBO process involves a warm-up stage, learning curve estimation, adaptive extraction of efficient and saturation points, evaluation to the efficient point for surrogate model updates, and a post-processing stage where promising configurations are evaluated to their saturation points to find the optimal configuration. This adaptive fidelity identification strategy is also shown to be generalizable, enabling the extension of any single-fidelity method to a multi-fidelity context.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO's performance was evaluated against several baseline and state-of-the-art multi-fidelity HPO methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. Experiments were conducted on widely used benchmarks for HPO and NAS: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. The primary validation method involved comparing the anytime performance of FastBO against these methods, demonstrating its rapid convergence to the global optimum.",
    "Limitations": "Not explicitly mentioned.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work involves refining and expanding FastBO to larger search spaces and distributed computing systems. This extension aims to improve its applicability and scalability in more complex and resource-intensive scenarios.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
