the incumbent value observed during the hpo process.\n        self.best_value_observed = np.NINF\n        # a set which will keep track of the hyperparameter configurations that diverge.\n        self.diverged_configs = set()\n\n        # info dict to drop every surrogate iteration\n        self.info_dict = dict()\n\n        # the start time for the overhead of every surrogate optimization iteration\n        # will be recorded here\n        self.suggest_time_duration = 0\n        # the total budget consumed so far\n        self.budget_spent = 0\n\n        self.output_path = output_path\n        self.dataset_name = dataset_name\n\n        self.no_improvement_threshold = int(self.max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * self.max_benchmark_epochs)\n        self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n\n\n    def _prepare_dataset_and_budgets(self) -> Dict[str, torch.Tensor]:\n        train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves = self.history_configurations()\n\n        train_examples = np.array(train_examples, dtype=np.single)\n        train_labels = np.array(train_labels, dtype=np.single)\n        train_budgets = np.array(train_budgets, dtype=np.single)\n        train_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(train_curves)\n        train_curves = np.array(train_curves, dtype=np.single)\n\n        # scale budgets to [0, 1]\n        train_budgets = train_budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs\n\n        train_examples = torch.tensor(train_examples)\n        train_labels = torch.tensor(train_labels)\n        train_budgets = torch.tensor(train_budgets)\n        train_curves = torch.tensor(train_curves)\n\n        train_examples = train_examples.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_labels = train_labels.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_budgets = train_budgets.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_curves = train_curves.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        data = {\n            'X_train': train_examples,\n            'train_budgets': train_budgets,\n            'train_curves': train_curves,\n            'y_train': train_labels,\n        }\n\n        return data\n\n    def _train_surrogate(self):\n        data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        self.logger.info(f'Started training the model')\n\n        self.model.train_pipeline(\n            data,\n            load_checkpoint=False,\n        )\n\n    def _predict(self) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, List, List]:\n        configurations, hp_indices, budgets, learning_curves = self.generate_candidate_configurations()\n        budgets = np.array(budgets, dtype=np.single)\n        non_scaled_budgets = copy.deepcopy(budgets)\n        # scale budgets to [0, 1]\n        budgets = budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs\n\n        configurations = np.array(configurations, dtype=np.single)\n        configurations = torch.tensor(configurations)\n        configurations = configurations.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        budgets = torch.tensor(budgets)\n        budgets = budgets.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        learning_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = np.array(learning_curves, dtype=np.single)\n        learning_curves = torch.tensor(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = learning_curves.to(device=self.dev)\n\n        train_data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        test_data = {\n            'X_test': configurations,\n            'test_budgets': budgets,\n            'test_curves': learning_curves,\n        }\n\n        mean_predictions, std_predictions = self.model.predict_pipeline(train_data, test_data)\n\n        return mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets\n\n    def suggest(self) -> Tuple[int, int]:\n        suggest_time_start = time.time()\n        if self.initial_random_index < len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            self.logger.info(\n                'Not enough configurations to build a model. '\n                'Returning randomly sampled configuration'\n            )\n\n            random_indice = self.init_conf_indices[self.initial_random_index]\n            budget = self.init_budgets[self.initial_random_index]\n            self.initial_random_index += 1\n\n            return random_indice, budget\n        else:\n            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets = self._predict()\n            best_prediction_index = self.find_suggested_config(\n                mean_predictions,\n                std_predictions,\n                non_scaled_budgets,\n            )\n            best_config_index = hp_indices[best_prediction_index]\n\n            if best_config_index in self.examples:\n                evaluated_budgets = self.examples[best_config_index]\n                max_budget = max(evaluated_budgets)\n                budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step\n                if budget > self.max_benchmark_epochs:\n                    budget = self.max_benchmark_epochs\n            else:\n                budget = self.fantasize_step\n\n        suggest_time_end = time.time()\n        self.suggest_time_duration = suggest_time_end - suggest_time_start\n\n        self.budget_spent += self.fantasize_step\n\n        if self.budget_spent > self.total_budget:\n            exit(0)\n\n        return best_config_index, budget\n\n    def observe(\n        self,\n        hp_index: int,\n        b: int,\n        learning_curve: np.ndarray,\n        alg_time: Optional[float] = None,\n    ):\n        score = learning_curve[-1]\n        if np.isnan(learning_curve).any():\n            self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, np.nan, 0)\n            self.diverged_configs.add(hp_index)\n            return\n\n        observe_time_start = time.time()\n\n        self.examples[hp_index] = np.arange(1, b + 1).tolist()\n        self.performances[hp_index] = learning_curve\n\n        if self.best_value_observed < score:\n            self.best_value_observed = score\n            self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n        else:\n            self.no_improvement_patience += 1\n\n        observe_time_end = time.time()\n        train_time_duration = 0\n\n        if self.initial_random_index >= len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            train_time_start = time.time()\n            if self.model is None:\n                self.model = DyHPO(\n                    self.surrogate_config,\n                    self.dev,\n                    self.dataset_name,\n                    self.output_path,\n                    self.seed,\n                )\n\n            if self.no_improvement_patience == self.no_improvement_threshold:\n                self.model.restart = True\n\n            self._train_surrogate()\n\n            train_time_end = time.time()\n            train_time_duration = train_time_end - train_time_start\n\n        observe_time_duration = observe_time_end - observe_time_start\n        total_duration = observe_time_duration + self.suggest_time_duration + train_time_duration\n        if alg_time is not None:\n            total_duration = total_duration + alg_time\n\n        self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, score, total_duration)\n\n    def prepare_examples(self, hp_indices: List) -> List[np.ndarray]:\n        examples = []\n        for hp_index in hp_indices:\n            examples.append(self.hp_candidates[hp_index])\n\n        return examples\n\n    def generate_candidate_configurations(\n        self,\n    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:\n        hp_indices = []\n        hp_budgets = []\n        learning_curves = []\n\n        for hp_index in range(0, self.hp_candidates.shape[0]):\n\n            if hp_index in self.examples:\n                budgets = self.examples[hp_index]\n                max_budget = max(budgets)\n                next_budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step\n                curve = self.performances[hp_index][:max_budget]\n                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(curve)\n                if difference_curve_length > 0:\n                    curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)\n            else:\n                next_budget = self.fantasize_step\n                curve = [0, 0, 0]\n\n            if next_budget <= self.max_benchmark_epochs:\n                hp_indices.append(hp_index)\n                hp_budgets.append(next_budget)\n                learning_curves.append(curve)\n\n        configurations = self.prepare_examples(hp_indices)\n\n        return configurations, hp_indices, hp_budgets, learning_curves\n\n    def history_configurations(\n        self,\n    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:\n        train_examples = []\n        train_labels = []\n        train_budgets = []\n        train_curves = []\n\n        for hp_index in self.examples:\n            budgets = self.examples[hp_index]\n            performances = self.performances[hp_index]\n            example = self.hp_candidates[hp_index]\n\n            for budget, performance in zip(budgets, performances):\n                train_examples.append(example)\n                train_budgets.append(budget)\n                train_labels.append(performance)\n                train_curve = performances[:budget - 1] if budget > 1 else [0.0]\n                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(train_curve)\n                if difference_curve_length > 0:\n                    train_curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)\n\n                train_curves.append(train_curve)\n\n        return train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves\n\n    def acq(\n        self,\n        best_value: float,\n        mean: float,\n        std: float,\n        explore_factor: Optional[float] = 0.25,\n        acq_fc: str = 'ei',\n    ) -> float:\n        if acq_fc == 'ei':\n            if std == 0:\n                return 0\n            z = (mean - best_value) / std\n            acq_value = (mean - best_value) * norm.cdf(z) + std * norm.pdf(z)\n        elif acq_fc == 'ucb':\n            acq_value = mean + explore_factor * std\n        elif acq_fc == 'thompson':\n            acq_value = np.random.normal(mean, std)\n        elif acq_fc == 'exploit':\n            acq_value = mean\n        else:\n            raise NotImplementedError(\n                f'Acquisition function {acq_fc} has not been'\n                f'implemented',\n            )\n\n        return acq_value\n\n    def find_suggested_config(\n        self,\n        mean_predictions: np.ndarray,\n        mean_stds: np.ndarray,\n        budgets: List,\n    ) -> int:\n        highest_acq_value = np.NINF\n        best_index = -1\n\n        index = 0\n        for mean_value, std in zip(mean_predictions, mean_stds):\n            budget = int(budgets[index])\n            best_value = self.calculate_fidelity_ymax(budget)\n            acq_value = self.acq(best_value, mean_value, std, acq_fc='ei')\n            if acq_value > highest_acq_value:\n                highest_acq_value = acq_value\n                best_index = index\n\n            index += 1\n\n        return best_index\n\n    def calculate_fidelity_ymax(self, fidelity: int):\n        exact_fidelity_config_values = []\n        lower_fidelity_config_values = []\n\n        for example_index in self.examples.keys():\n            try:\n                performance = self.performances[example_index][fidelity - 1]\n                exact_fidelity_config_values.append(performance)\n            except IndexError:\n                learning_curve = self.performances[example_index]\n                # The hyperparameter was not evaluated until fidelity, or more.\n                # Take the maximum value from the curve.\n                lower_fidelity_config_values.append(max(learning_curve))\n\n        if len(exact_fidelity_config_values) > 0:\n            # lowest error corresponds to best value\n            best_value = max(exact_fidelity_config_values)\n        else:\n            best_value = max(lower_fidelity_config_values)\n\n        return best_value\n\n    def preprocess_hp_candidates(self) -> List:\n        log_hp_candidates = []\n\n        for hp_candidate in self.hp_candidates:\n            new_hp_candidate = []\n            for index, hp_value in enumerate(hp_candidate):\n                new_hp_candidate.append(math.log(hp_value) if self.log_indicator[index] else hp_value)\n\n            log_hp_candidates.append(new_hp_candidate)\n\n        log_hp_candidates = np.array(log_hp_candidates)\n        # scaler for the hp configurations\n\n        log_hp_candidates = self.scaler.fit_transform(log_hp_candidates)\n\n        return log_hp_candidates\n\n    @staticmethod\n    def patch_curves_to_same_length(curves):\n        max_curve_length = 0\n        for curve in curves:\n            if len(curve) > max_curve_length:\n                max_curve_length = len(curve)\n\n        for curve in curves:\n            difference = max_curve_length - len(curve)\n            if difference > 0:\n                curve.extend([0.0] * difference)\n\n        return curves\n\nclass FeatureExtractor(nn.Module):\n    def __init__(self, configuration):\n        super(FeatureExtractor, self).__init__()\n\n        self.configuration = configuration\n\n        self.nr_layers = configuration['nr_layers']\n        self.act_func = nn.LeakyReLU()\n        initial_features = configuration['nr_initial_features'] + 1\n        self.fc1 = nn.Linear(initial_features, configuration['layer1_units'])\n        self.bn1 = nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration['layer1_units'])\n        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):\n            setattr(\n                self,\n                f'fc{i + 1}',\n                nn.Linear(configuration[f'layer{i - 1}_units'], configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),\n            )\n            setattr(\n                self,\n                f'bn{i + 1}',\n                nn.BatchNorm1d(configuration[f'layer{i}_units']),\n            )\n\n\n        setattr(\n            self,\n            f'fc{self.nr_layers}',\n            nn.Linear(\n                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers - 1}_units'] +\n                configuration['cnn_nr_channels'],\n                configuration[f'layer{self.nr_layers}_units']\n            ),\n        )\n        self.cnn = nn.Sequential(\n            nn.Conv1d(in_channels=1, kernel_size=(configuration['cnn_kernel_size'],), out_channels=4),\n            nn.AdaptiveMaxPool1d(1),\n        )\n\n    def forward(self, x, budgets, learning_curves):\n        budgets = torch.unsqueeze(budgets, dim=1)\n        x = cat((x, budgets), dim=1)\n        x = self.fc1(x)\n        x = self.act_func(self.bn1(x))\n\n        for i in range(2, self.nr_layers):\n            x = self.act_func(\n                getattr(self, f'bn{i}')(\n                    getattr(self, f'fc{i}')(\n                        x\n                    )\n                )\n            )\n\n        learning_curves = torch.unsqueeze(learning_curves, 1)\n        lc_features = self.cnn(learning_curves)\n        lc_features = torch.squeeze(lc_features, 2)\n\n        x = cat((x, lc_features), dim=1)\n        x = self.act_func(getattr(self, f'fc{self.nr_layers}')(x))\n\n        return x\n\n\nclass GPRegressionModel(gpytorch.models.ExactGP):\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        train_x: torch.Tensor,\n        train_y: torch.Tensor,\n        likelihood: gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood,\n    ):\n        super(GPRegressionModel, self).__init__(train_x, train_y, likelihood)\n\n        self.mean_module = gpytorch.means.ConstantMean()\n        self.covar_module = gpytorch.kernels.ScaleKernel(gpytorch.kernels.RBFKernel())\n\n    def forward(self, x):\n        mean_x = self.mean_module(x)\n        covar_x = self.covar_module(x)\n\n        return gpytorch.distributions.MultivariateNormal(mean_x, covar_x)\n\n\nclass DyHPO:\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        configuration: Dict,\n        device: torch.device,\n        dataset_name: str = 'unknown',\n        output_path: str = '.',\n        seed: int = 11,\n    ):\n        super(DyHPO, self).__init__()\n        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(configuration)\n        self.batch_size = configuration['batch_size']\n        self.nr_epochs = configuration['nr_epochs']\n        self.early_stopping_patience = configuration['nr_patience_epochs']\n        self.refine_epochs = 50\n        self.dev = device\n        self.seed = seed\n        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \\\n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(\n                configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units']\n            )\n\n        self.model.to(self.dev)\n        self.likelihood.to(self.dev)\n        self.feature_extractor.to(self.dev)\n\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n\n        self.configuration = configuration\n        self.initial_nr_points = 10\n        self.iterations = 0\n        self.restart = True\n\n        self.logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)\n\n        self.checkpoint_path = os.path.join(\n            output_path,\n            'checkpoints',\n            f'{dataset_name}',\n            f'{self.seed}',\n        )\n\n        os.makedirs(self.checkpoint_path, exist_ok=True)\n\n        self.checkpoint_file = os.path.join(\n            self.checkpoint_path,\n            'checkpoint.pth'\n        )\n\n    def restart_optimization(self):\n        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(self.configuration).to(self.dev)\n        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \\\n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(\n                self.configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units'],\n            )\n\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n\n    def get_model_likelihood_mll(\n        self,\n        train_size: int,\n    ) -> Tuple[GPRegressionModel, gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood, gpytorch.mlls.ExactMarginalLogLikelihood]:\n        train_x = torch.ones(train_size, train_size).to(self.dev)\n        train_y = torch.ones(train_size).to(self.dev)\n\n        likelihood = gpytorch.likelihoods.GaussianLikelihood().to(self.dev)\n        model = GPRegressionModel(train_x=train_x, train_y=train_y, likelihood=likelihood).to(self.dev)\n        mll = gpytorch.mlls.ExactMarginalLogLikelihood(likelihood, model).to(self.dev)\n\n        return model, likelihood, mll\n\n    def train_pipeline(self, data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor], load_checkpoint: bool = False):\n        self.iterations += 1\n        self.logger.debug(f'Starting iteration: {self.iterations}')\n        weights_changed = False\n\n        if load_checkpoint:\n            try:\n                self.load_checkpoint()\n            except FileNotFoundError:\n                self.logger.error(f'No checkpoint file found at: {self.checkpoint_file}'\n                                  f'Training the GP from the beginning')\n\n        self.model.train()\n        self.likelihood.train()\n        self.feature_extractor.train()\n\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n\n        X_train = data['X_train']\n        train_budgets = data['train_budgets']\n        train_curves = data['train_curves']\n        y_train = data['y_train']\n\n        initial_state = self.get_state()\n        training_errored = False\n\n        if self.restart:\n            self.restart_optimization()\n            nr_epochs = self.nr_epochs\n            if self.initial_nr_points <= self.iterations:\n                self.restart = False\n        else:\n            nr_epochs = self.refine_epochs\n\n        mse = 0.0\n\n        for epoch_nr in range(0, nr_epochs):\n\n            nr_examples_batch = X_train.size(dim=0)\n            if nr_examples_batch == 1:\n                continue\n\n            self.optimizer.zero_grad()\n\n            projected_x = self.feature_extractor(X_train, train_budgets, train_curves)\n            self.model.set_train_data(projected_x, y_train, strict=False)\n            output = self.model(projected_x)\n\n            try:\n                loss = -self.mll(output, self.model.train_targets)\n                loss_value = loss.detach().to('cpu').item()\n                mse = gpytorch.metrics.mean_squared_error(output, self.model.train_targets)\n                self.logger.debug(\n                    f'Epoch {epoch_nr} - MSE {mse:.5f}, '\n                    f'Loss: {loss_value:.3f}, '\n                    f'lengthscale: {self.model.covar_module.base_kernel.lengthscale.item():.3f}, '\n                    f'noise: {self.model.likelihood.noise.item():.3f}, '\n                )\n                loss.backward()\n                self.optimizer.step()\n            except Exception as training_error:\n                self.logger.error(f'The following error happened while training: {training_error}')\n                self.restart = True\n                training_errored = True\n                break\n\n        if training_errored:\n            self.save_checkpoint(initial_state)\n            self.load_checkpoint()\n\n    def predict_pipeline(\n        self,\n        train_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],\n        test_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],\n    ) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n        self.model.eval()\n        self.feature_extractor.eval()\n        self.likelihood.eval()\n\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            projected_train_x = self.feature_extractor(\n                train_data['X_train'],\n                train_data['train_budgets'],\n                train_data['train_curves'],\n            )\n            self.model.set_train_data(inputs=projected_train_x, targets=train_data['y_train'], strict=False)\n            projected_test_x = self.feature_extractor(\n                test_data['X_test'],\n                test_data['test_budgets'],\n                test_data['test_curves'],\n            )\n            preds = self.likelihood(self.model(projected_test_x))\n\n        means = preds.mean.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )\n        stds = preds.stddev.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )\n\n        return means, stds",
    "Experiment Result": "Overall HPO Settings (from `DyHPOAlgorithm` and `main_experiment.py`):\n- Seed: controlled by command-line arg `--index`, typically `np.arange(10)`. Default `11` in `DyHPOAlgorithm` if not specified.\n- Max Benchmark Epochs: `benchmark.max_budget` (51 for both LCBench and TaskSet benchmarks).\n- Fantasize Step: `args.fantasize_step` (default `1`).\n- Minimization Problem: `True` for `taskset` benchmark (loss), `False` for `lcbench` benchmark (accuracy).\n- Total HPO Budget: `args.budget_limit` (default `1000`).\n- Initial Configurations: `initial_configurations_nr = 1` (randomly chosen).\n- Initial Budget per Configuration: `conf_individual_budget = 1`.\n- Fraction of Random Configurations: `fraction_random_configs = 0.1`.\n- No Improvement Threshold for Model Restart: `int(self.max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * self.max_benchmark_epochs)`.     "Title": "Gradient Descent: The Ultimate Optimizer",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces an elegant method for automatically computing hypergradients using a simple modification to backpropagation. This enables online adaptation of all optimizer hyperparameters (e.g., step size, momentum coefficients) across various optimizers without manual differentiation. A key contribution is the ability to recursively stack these 'hyperoptimizers' to create arbitrarily tall 'towers of optimizers,' which empirically increases robustness to the initial human-chosen hyperparameters and significantly reduces the burden of manual tuning.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves applying automatic differentiation (AD) to compute gradients of the loss function with respect to hyperparameters (hypergradients). This is achieved by carefully controlling gradient flow during backpropagation, specifically by refraining from detaching hyperparameters (like the learning rate `alpha`) from the computation graph, while detaching their parents. This allows `alpha` to accumulate its own gradient. The technique generalizes to other optimizers (e.g., Adam, AdaGrad, RMSProp) and all their hyperparameters by simply treating them similarly to `alpha`. Specific considerations include clamping hyperparameters like Adam's `beta1` and `beta2` to their valid domains (0,1) using a scaled sigmoid, and initializing Adam's `v0` to `epsilon` instead of 0 to avoid division by zero. Recursion is implemented by refactoring the hyperoptimizer's `step` method to apply another optimizer to its own hyperparameters, enabling the creation of multi-level 'towers of optimizers'.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on a single NVIDIA TITAN Xp GPU across various benchmarks. For SGD, Adam, AdaGrad, and RMSProp, a neural network with one fully-connected hidden layer (128 units, tanh activations) was trained on the MNIST dataset with a batch size of 256 for 30 epochs (SGD) or 5 epochs (Adam/AdaGrad/RMSProp), with statistics over 3 runs. For larger scale evaluation, a ResNet-20 was trained on the CIFAR-10 dataset for 200-500 epochs, testing robustness to initial step size and momentum, and comparing against hand-engineered learning rate schedules. A character-level RNN (2-layer LSTM with 128 hidden nodes) was trained on the Tolstoy dataset for 50,000 gradient descent steps using Adam, evaluating performance across different initial learning rates. Higher-order hyperoptimization was tested on MLP (MNIST), ResNet (CIFAR-10), and Char-RNN (Tolstoy) benchmarks with increasing stack heights and varying initial base-level step sizes. A scalability test involved fine-tuning a pretrained ResNet-152 on the Caltech-256 dataset. Runtime scaling with stack height was also measured.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation is that the hyperoptimizers cannot yet handle initial hyperparameters that are set far too high, leading to system instability and divergence before the hyperoptimizer can take effect. The implementation requires careful computation graph management to avoid bugs like memory leaks (e.g., loggers must detach what is logged). Additionally, certain built-in PyTorch modules (like the LSTM) cannot be used directly because they silently modify the computation graph, which can lead to incorrect gradients with the proposed system.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research is needed to design hyperoptimizers that are robust to initial hyperparameters set far too high, potentially involving a deeper theoretical analysis of convergence. Further work is also required to design differentiable programming languages that allow methods like theirs to be expressed in a more modular and composable manner, minimizing the risk of computation graph-related bugs. The authors also hope that advances in online hyperparameter tuning, enabled by their work, can reduce the significant energy impact of deep learning training and hyperparameter searches.",

    "Title": "Implicit differentiation of Lasso-type models for hyperparameter optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces an efficient implicit differentiation algorithm, without matrix inversion, specifically designed for hyperparameter optimization of non-smooth Lasso-type problems. It addresses the challenges of traditional methods (grid-search's exponential complexity, automatic differentiation's high memory consumption, implicit differentiation's numerical instability and smooth function assumption). The proposed method leverages the sparsity of Lasso solutions, scales to high-dimensional data, and empirically outperforms various state-of-the-art hyperparameter optimization techniques (including grid-search, random search, Bayesian optimization, and other iterative differentiation methods) for optimizing held-out validation error or the Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE). Key technical contributions include proving that forward iterative differentiation of Block Coordinate Descent (BCD) converges to the true gradient linearly once the support is identified, and developing Algorithm 2 which decouples the computation of regression coefficients from the Jacobian, avoiding the need to solve potentially ill-conditioned linear systems.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology casts hyperparameter optimization as a bi-level optimization problem. For Lasso-type models, which involve non-smooth regularization, the authors derive an implicit differentiation algorithm that does not require direct matrix inversion. It exploits the fixed-point iterations induced by proximal Block Coordinate Descent (BCD) algorithms. Specifically, for Lasso and weighted Lasso, they differentiate the fixed-point equation to obtain closed-form solutions for the weak Jacobian (Proposition 1), highlighting its sparse structure. The core is Algorithm 2 (Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation), which operates in two main steps: first, it computes the regression coefficients and identifies their support using an existing state-of-the-art solver; second, it computes the Jacobian using forward differentiation recursion steps restricted to the identified support. This decoupling and restriction to the support significantly reduce computational cost and improve stability compared to solving full linear systems or generic iterative differentiation methods. The method's convergence properties (linear convergence of the Jacobian once the support is identified) are established in Proposition 2.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental evaluation employed a consistent vanilla Block Coordinate Descent (BCD) algorithm (Algorithm 5) for all methods to solve the inner Lasso/weighted Lasso problems, with a stopping criterion based on relative function value change. Hypergradient-based methods used a line-search strategy. Competitors included other gradient-based methods (Implicit Differentiation requiring linear system inversion, Forward Iterative Differentiation, Backward Iterative Differentiation) and non-gradient-based methods (Grid-search, Random-search, Lattice Hypercube sampling, Bayesian optimization). Performance was evaluated using two criteria: held-out loss (splitting real datasets into train/validation/test sets) and Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE) using a weakly differentiable Monte-Carlo approximation for simulated data. Datasets included high-dimensional real-world data (rcv1 (p=19,959), 20news (p=130,107), finance (p=1,668,737)) and simulated data (n=100, p varying from 200 to 10,000). The method was also tested on an estimator with a non-convex penalty (MCP) and a toy example designed to exhibit non-unique inner solutions. Metrics included objective function distance to optimum, test set loss, and relative Mean Squared Error (MSE) for coefficient estimation, along with running time.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical guarantees for the proposed implicit differentiation algorithm (e.g., uniqueness of Jacobian derivation and convergence) primarily apply to convex Lasso-type problems with unique solutions. While the method numerically performs well, the theory does not cover non-convex penalty functions like MCP, where estimators may not be continuous with respect to hyperparameters. For cases where the Lasso solution is not unique (which is typically rare or has zero Lebesgue measure), the function mapping hyperparameters to coefficients might be non-continuous, potentially invalidating gradient-based methods, although the algorithm can still be applied without theoretical guarantees. The SURE criterion, used for hyperparameter optimization when held-out data is not suitable, requires prior knowledge of the noise variance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A primary future research direction is extending the theoretical framework and application of the proposed algorithm to handle non-convex penalties and other non-convex formulations, such as the Minimax Concave Penalty (MCP). The paper also suggests investigating other model formulations like Elastic-Net. Furthermore, the two-step nature of the proposed algorithm allows for the integration of state-of-the-art Lasso solvers that use advanced techniques like active sets or screening rules. Future work could explore how to effectively integrate these solvers, especially considering that such advanced techniques can introduce discontinuities with respect to hyperparameters, posing challenges for gradient-based approaches.",

    "Experiment Result": "The experiments employ a variety of datasets, both synthetic and real-world, including `make_classification`, `make_regression`, `make_correlated_data` for synthetic scenarios, and `rcv1.binary`, `rcv1_train`, `real-sim`, `news20`, `leukemia`, `finance`, `mnist`, `usps`, `sector_scale`, `aloi`, `gisette`, `covtype` (from libsvmdata) and MNE MEG sample data for real-world applications. Data splitting strategies include held-out validation (e.g., first half for training, second half for validation) and K-fold cross-validation (`KFold(n_splits=5, shuffle=True, random_state=42)`). Inner optimization problems (e.g., Lasso, ElasticNet, Sparse Logistic Regression, SVM) are solved using `celer` or `sklearn.linear_model` estimators, typically with `fit_intercept=False` and `warm_start=True`. The inner solver tolerance (`tol`) varies from `1e-3` to `1e-16`, with `1e-8` being common, and maximum iterations (`max_iter`) ranging from `50` to `100,000`. Hyperparameter ranges for regularization parameters (e.g., `alpha` for Lasso) are typically calculated relative to `alpha_max` (the smallest value that makes all coefficients zero), with `alpha_min` being `1e-2` to `1e-6` times `alpha_max`. Starting points (`alpha0`) for gradient-based methods are often set as `alpha_max / 10` or similar proportions. The core implicit forward differentiation method (`ImplicitForward`) uses specific Jacobian computation parameters: `tol_jac` typically set between `1e-3` and `1e-8` (or `1e-11` for high-precision comparisons), and `n_iter_jac` from `100` to `100,000`. Outer loop optimizers include `LineSearch` (`n_outer` typically 10-30), `GradientDescent` (`n_outer` 10-100, sometimes with adaptive `p_grad_norm` and `tol_decrease`), and `Adam` (`n_outer` around 10, with `lr=0.11`). For comparative studies, zero-order methods like `Grid-search` (with `n_alphas` from 10 to 30, or `15x15` grids for 2D hyperparameters) and `Random-search` or `Bayesian optimization` (both via `Hyperopt` with `max_evals` from 10 to 100) are also employed. Progress is monitored using a `Monitor` class that records outer criterion values, computation times, hyperparameter values, gradients, and accuracy metrics."
}{
    "Title": "Stochastic Marginal Likelihood Gradients using Neural Tangent Kernels",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of scaling gradient-based hyperparameter optimization using Bayesian model selection in deep learning. Previous methods for estimating marginal likelihood gradients required a full pass through the dataset, limiting scalability. This work introduces novel lower bounds to the linearized Laplace approximation of the marginal likelihood, which are amenable to stochastic-gradient-based optimization. These bounds allow for a trade-off between estimation accuracy and computational complexity, accelerating hyperparameter optimization by up to 25-fold. The bounds are derived using the function-space form of the linearized Laplace, estimable via the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK), and also extend to parametric approximations like KFAC.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves optimizing hyperparameters by maximizing the marginal likelihood, approximated using the linearized Laplace (LA) method. The key innovation is the derivation of lower bounds for the log marginal likelihood that enable stochastic gradient estimation. This includes: 1. Parametric structured lower bounds (Theorem 1) demonstrating that block-diagonal and diagonal Generalized Gauss-Newton (GGN) approximations are valid lower bounds. 2. Data subset kernel bounds (Theorem 2) based on partitioning the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) into batches of input-output pairs, allowing for unbiased stochastic estimates. 3. Parametric data subset bounds (Theorem 3 and Corollary 3.1) showing equivalence between NTK-based and parametric (e.g., KFAC) approaches on data subsets, facilitating stochastic optimization with structured GGN approximations. Partitioning strategies, such as output-wise partitioning or grouping inputs by labels, are explored to improve bound tightness and efficiency. An algorithm (Algorithm 1) is provided for stochastic marginal likelihood estimation using these bounds.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The estimators were validated on various datasets and neural network architectures. Illustrative experiments were conducted on subsets of MNIST (1000 random digits) and rotated MNIST using a small 3-layer convolutional neural network (approx. 16,000 parameters) to demonstrate bound tightness for prior precision and rotational invariance hyperparameters. Benchmarking experiments involved CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 datasets with a Wide ResNet 16-4 and TinyImagenet with a ResNet-50 (approx. 23 million parameters) using Fixup initialization. Hyperparameters optimized included layer-wise prior precisions (weight decay) and affine invariances. Comparisons were made against the full-batch KFAC-Laplace (state-of-the-art baseline), Augerino, and neural network partitioning methods. Evaluation metrics included log marginal likelihood, test log likelihood, test accuracy, and runtime (analyzed for Pareto-efficiency). Experiments used Adam for network parameters and SGD for hyperparameter gradients with specific learning rates and burn-in periods.",
    "Limitations": "Existing gradient-based Bayesian model selection methods suffered from limited scalability due to the requirement of a full-dataset pass for each hyperparameter gradient. The empirical Bayes approach of optimizing marginal likelihood can lead to overfitting if the number of hyperparameters becomes too large. Naively computing the log-determinant on data subsets can lead to an uncontrolled upper bound. While the proposed bounds mitigate this, very small subset sizes can lead to optimization failures. The diagonal approximation of the Hessian can catastrophically fail due to high slack in the bound. Class-wise partitioning, while potentially making bounds tighter, tends to incur a larger variance in the estimated bound. Furthermore, the full-batch KFAC-Laplace method (baseline) was found to be intractable for larger datasets like CIFAR-100 and TinyImagenet due to memory and computational costs.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could focus on improving the tightness of the derived bounds, for instance, by developing more effective partitioning strategies for the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK). Investigating methods that track (anti-)correlation between inputs in the NTK to optimally group them during training, similar to inducing point optimization, is another promising direction. Additionally, the fast and scalable estimators developed in this work could be applied to large-scale Bayesian linear models.",
    "Experiment Code": "import logging\nfrom time import time\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\nfrom torch.nn.utils.convert_parameters import vector_to_parameters\nfrom torch.optim import Adam, criterion = MSELoss(reduction='mean')\n        log_sigma_noise_init = np.log(sigma_noise_init)\n        log_sigma_noise = log_sigma_noise_init * torch.ones(1, device=device)\n        log_sigma_noise.requires_grad = True\n        hyperparameters.append(log_sigma_noise)\n\n    # set up model optimizer and scheduler\n    optimizer = get_model_optimizer(optimizer, model, lr)\n    scheduler = get_scheduler(scheduler, optimizer, train_loader, n_epochs, lr, lr_min)\n\n    n_steps = ((n_epochs - n_epochs_burnin) // marglik_frequency) * n_hypersteps_prior\n    hyper_optimizer = Adam(hyperparameters, lr=lr_hyp)\n    hyper_scheduler = CosineAnnealingLR(hyper_optimizer, n_steps, eta_min=lr_hyp_min)\n    if optimize_aug:\n        logging.info('MARGLIK: optimize augmentation.')\n        aug_optimizer = Adam(augmenter.parameters(), lr=lr_aug)\n        n_steps = ((n_epochs - n_epochs_burnin) // marglik_frequency) * (n_hypersteps if stochastic_grad else 1)\n        aug_scheduler       epoch_nll = 0\n        epoch_log = dict(epoch=epoch)\n\n        # standard NN training per batch\n        torch.cuda.empty_cache()\n        for X, y in train_loader:\n            X, y = crit_factor = 1 / temperature / (2 * sigma_noise.square())\n            else:\n                crit_factor = 1 / temperature\n            prior_prec = torch.exp(log_prior_prec).detach()\n            delta = expand_prior_precision(prior_prec, model)\n\n            # fit data\n            time_fwd = time()\n            if method == 'lila':\n                f = model(X).mean(dim=1)\n parameters_to_vector(model.parameters())\n            loss = criterion(f, y) + (0.5 * (delta * theta) @ theta) / N / crit_factor\n            loss.backward()\n            optimizer.step()\n            epoch_time_fit += time() - time_fit # log total time bwd fit in epoch\n\n            epoch_loss += loss.cpu().item() / len(train_loader)\n            epoch_nll += criterion(f.detach(), y).item() / len(train_loader)\n            if likelihood == 'regression':\n                epoch_perf += (f.detach() - y).square().sum() / N\n            else:\n                epoch_perf += torch.sum(torch.argmax(f.detach(), dim=-1) == y).item() / N\n            scheduler.step()\n\n        losses.append(epoch_loss)\n        logging.info('MAP memory allocated: ' +     "Title": "AUTOMATA: Gradient Based Data Subset Selection for Compute-Efficient Hyper-parameter Tuning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of time-consuming hyper-parameter optimization (HPO) for deep neural networks by proposing AUTOMATA, a gradient-based data subset selection framework. The central insight is that using informative data subsets for model training during HPO can significantly accelerate the process. AUTOMATA empirically demonstrates speedups of 3x-30x in hyper-parameter tuning, while achieving comparable performance to hyper-parameters found using the entire dataset, thereby reducing computational costs, running times, and CO2 emissions. It is the first work to study the role of intelligent data subset selection for hyper-parameter tuning, showing that small informative data subsets (1%-30%) enable faster configuration evaluations.",
    "Methodology": "AUTOMATA is a three-component framework: a hyper-parameter search algorithm (e.g., Random Search, TPE), a gradient-based subset selection (GSS) algorithm for efficient configuration evaluation, and a hyper-parameter scheduling algorithm (e.g., Hyperband, ASHA) for early stopping. The core of AUTOMATA is the GSS, which selects a subset S and its weight vector w such that the weighted subset loss gradient best approximates the entire training loss gradient. This optimization problem, formulated as minimizing the L2 norm of the gradient difference plus a regularization term, is approximately submodular and solved using a greedy algorithm called Orthogonal Matching Pursuit (OMP). A 'per-batch' subset selection is employed for efficiency, where a subset of mini-batches is selected instead of individual data points. Model warm-starting is also used for ASHA scheduler experiments to provide informative loss gradients for selection.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted across text, image, and tabular domains using various real-world datasets. Text datasets included SST2, SST5, glue-SST2, and TREC6, trained with an LSTM model with GloVe embeddings. Image datasets included CIFAR10, CIFAR100, and SVHN, trained with ResNet18 and ResNet50 models. Tabular datasets included DNA, SATIMAGE, LETTER, and CONNECT-4, trained with a multi-layer perceptron. Baselines included RANDOM (random subset), CRAIG (gradient-based subset from prior work), and FULL (entire dataset). Hyper-parameter search algorithms were Random Search and TPE, and scheduling algorithms were Hyperband and ASHA. Experiments used subset size fractions of 1%, 5%, 10%, and 30%. Models were trained for 20 epochs (text), 300 epochs (image), and 200 epochs (tabular), with subset selection occurring every 5, 20, and 10 epochs respectively. Final training on the full dataset with the best-found hyper-parameters was performed for all methods except FULL.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation of AUTOMATA is the uncertainty regarding the minimum subset size required to achieve no performance loss. In scenarios where no performance degradation is desired, the approach currently relies on larger subset sizes (e.g., 10%, 30%), rather than adaptively determining the optimal small subset size.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research will focus on adaptively changing subset sizes based on model performance for each configuration. This aims to remove the current dependency on a fixed subset size, potentially allowing for even greater efficiency gains while maintaining performance.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: configs/SL/config_gradmatchpb-warm_cifar10.py\nContent:\n# Learning setting\nconfig = dict(setting=\"SL\",\n              is_reg = False,\n \"time\"],\n                              return_args=[]\n                              )\n              )\n\nFile Path: cords/selectionstrategies/SL/gradmatchstrategy.py\nContent:\nimport math\nimport time\nimport torch\nimport numpy as np\nfrom .dataselectionstrategy import DataSelectionStrategy\nfrom ..helpers import OrthogonalMP_REG_Parallel, OrthogonalMP_REG, OrthogonalMP_REG_Parallel_V1\nfrom torch.utils.data import Subset, DataLoader\n\n\nclass GradMatchStrategy(DataSelectionStrategy):\n    \"\"\"\n    Implementation of GradMatch Strategy from the paper :footcite:`pmlr-v139-killamsetty21a` for supervised learning frameworks.\n\n    GradMatch strategy tries to solve the optimization problem given below:\n\n    .. math::\n        \\min_{\\mathbf{w}, S: |S| \\leq k} \\Vert \\sum_{i \\in S} w_i \\nabla_{\\theta}L_T^i(\\theta) -  \\nabla_{\\theta}L(\\theta)\\Vert\n\n    In the above equation, :math:`\\mathbf{w}` denotes the weight vector that contains the weights for each data instance, :math:`\\mathcal{U}` training set where :math:`(x^i, y^i)` denotes the :math:`i^{th}` training data point and label respectively,\n    :math:`L_T` denotes the training loss, :math:`L` denotes either training loss or validation loss depending on the parameter valid,\n    :math:`S` denotes the data subset selected at each round, and :math:`k` is the budget for the subset.\n\n    The above optimization problem is solved using the Orthogonal Matching Pursuit(OMP) algorithm.\n\n    Parameters\n\t----------\n    trainloader: class\n        Loading the training data using pytorch DataLoader\n    valloader: class\n        Loading the validation data using pytorch DataLoader\n    model: class\n        Model architecture used for training\n    loss: class\n        PyTorch loss function for training\n    eta: float\n        Learning rate. Step size for the one step gradient update\n    device: str\n        The device being utilized - cpu | cuda\n    num_classes: int\n        The number of target classes in the dataset\n    linear_layer: bool\n        Apply linear transformation to the data\n    selection_type: str\n        Type of selection -\n        - 'PerClass': PerClass method is where OMP algorithm is applied on each class data points seperately.\n        - 'PerBatch': PerBatch method is where OMP algorithm is applied on each minibatch data points.\n        - 'PerClassPerGradient': PerClassPerGradient method is same as PerClass but we use the gradient corresponding to classification layer of that class only.\n    logger : class\n        - logger object for logging the information\n    valid : bool\n        If valid==True, we use validation dataset gradient sum in OMP otherwise we use training dataset (default: False)\n    v1 : bool\n        If v1==True, we use newer version of OMP solver that is more accurate\n    lam : float\n        Regularization constant of OMP solver\n    eps : float\n        Epsilon parameter to which the above optimization problem is solved using OMP algorithm\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, trainloader, valloader, model, loss,\n                 eta, device, num_classes, linear_layer,\n                 selection_type, logger, valid=False, v1=True, lam=0, eps=1e-4):\n        \"\"\"\n        Constructor method\n        \"\"\"\n        super().__init__(trainloader, valloader, model, num_classes, linear_layer, loss, device, logger)\n        self.eta = eta  # step size for the one step gradient update\n        self.device = device\n        self.init_out = list()\n        self.init_l1 = list()\n        self.selection_type = selection_type\n        self.valid = valid\n        self.lam = lam\n        self.eps = eps\n        self.v1 = v1\n\n    def ompwrapper(self, X, Y, bud):\n        if self.device == \"cpu\":\n            reg = OrthogonalMP_REG(X.numpy(), Y.numpy(), nnz=bud, positive=True, lam=0)\n            ind = np.nonzero(reg)[0]\n        else:\n            if self.v1:\n                reg = OrthogonalMP_REG_Parallel_V1(X, Y, nnz=bud,\n                                                 positive=True, lam=self.lam,\n                                                 tol=self.eps, device=self.device)\n            else:\n                reg = OrthogonalMP_REG_Parallel(X, Y, nnz=bud,\n                                                positive=True, lam=self.lam,\n                                                tol=self.eps, device=self.device)\n            ind = torch.nonzero(reg).view(-1)\n        return ind.tolist(), reg[ind].tolist()\n\n    def select(self, budget, model_params):\n        \"\"\"\n        Apply OMP Algorithm for data selection\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        budget: int\n            The number of data points to be selected\n        model_params: OrderedDict\n            Python dictionary object containing models parameters\n\n        Returns\n        ----------\n        idxs: list\n            List containing indices of the best datapoints,\n        gammas: weights tensors\n            Tensor containing weights of each instance\n        \"\"\"\n        omp_start_time = time.time()\n        self.update_model(model_params)\n\n        if self.selection_type == 'PerClass':\n            self.get_labels(valid=self.valid)\n            idxs = []\n            gammas = []\n            for i in range(self.num_classes):\n                trn_subset_idx = torch.where(self.trn_lbls == i)[0].tolist()\n                trn_data_sub = Subset(self.trainloader.dataset, trn_subset_idx)\n                self.pctrainloader = DataLoader(trn_data_sub, batch_size=self.trainloader.batch_size,\n                                                shuffle=False, pin_memory=True, collate_fn=self.trainloader.collate_fn)\n                if self.valid:\n                    val_subset_idx = torch.where(self.val_lbls == i)[0].tolist()\n                    val_data_sub = Subset(self.valloader.dataset, val_subset_idx)\n                    self.pcvalloader = DataLoader(val_data_sub, batch_size=self.trainloader.batch_size,\n                                                  shuffle=False, pin_memory=True, collate_fn=self.trainloader.collate_fn)\n\n                self.compute_gradients(self.valid, perBatch=False, perClass=True)\n                trn_gradients = self.grads_per_elem\n                if self.valid:\n                    sum_val_grad = torch.sum(self.val_grads_per_elem, dim=0)\n                else:\n                    sum_val_grad = torch.sum(trn_gradients, dim=0)\n                idxs_temp, gammas_temp = self.ompwrapper(torch.transpose(trn_gradients, 0, 1),\n                                                         sum_val_grad,\n                                                         math.ceil(budget * len(trn_subset_idx) / self.N_trn))\n                idxs.extend(list(np.array(trn_subset_idx)[idxs_temp]))\n                gammas.extend(gammas_temp)\n\n        elif self.selection_type == 'PerBatch':\n            self.compute_gradients(self.valid, perBatch=True, perClass=False)\n            idxs = []\n            gammas = []\n            trn_gradients = self.grads_per_elem\n            if self.valid:\n                sum_val_grad = torch.sum(self.val_grads_per_elem, dim=0)\n            else:\n numpy.linalg import inv\nfrom numpy.linalg import norm\nfrom scipy import sparse as sp\nfrom scipy.linalg import lstsq\nfrom scipy.optimize import nnls\nimport torch\n\n\n# NOTE: Textbook Primal-Dual IPM: Boyd & Vandenberghe, ``Chapter 11: Interior-point Methods,\" Convex Optimization, 2004.\n# NOTE: Works on toy problems but struggles in word embedding recovery setting (n>10000).\ndef NonnegativeBP(A, b, x0=None, tol=1E-4, niter=100, biter=32):\n    '''solves min |x|_1 s.t. Ax=b,x>=0 using a Primal-Dual Interior Point Method\n    Args:\n      A: design matrix of size (d, n)\n      b: measurement vector of length d\n      x0: starting point; if None sets x0=A^T(AA^T)^(-1)b\n      tol: solver tolerance\n      niter: maximum length of central path\n      biter: maximum number of steps in backtracking line search\n    Returns:\n      vector of length n\n    '''\n\n    AT = A.T\n    d, n = A.shape\n    alpha = 0.01\n    beta = 0.5\n    mu = 20\n    e = np.ones(n)\n\n    if x0 is None:\n        x = tol / np.sqrt(n) * e\n        x += nnls(A, b - A.dot(x))[0]\n    else:\n        x = np.copy(x0)\n    lam = 1.0 / x\n    v = -A.dot(lam)\n    t = mu * d\n    rp = A.dot(x) - b\n    rd = 1.0 - lam + AT.dot(v)\n\n    for i in range(niter):\n\n        oot = 1.0 / t\n        rc = lam * x - oot\n        resnorm = np.sqrt(norm(rd) ** 2 + norm(rc) ** 2 + norm(rp) ** 2)\n\n        try:\n            dv = solve(A.dot(AT * (x / lam)[:, np.newaxis]), rp - A.dot((rc + x * rd) / lam), assume_a='pos')\n        except np.linalg.linalg.LinAlgError:\n            return x\n        dlam = AT.dot(dv) + rd\n        dx = -(rc + x * dlam) / lam\n\n        ind = np.less(dlam, 0.0)\n        s = 0.99 * min(1.0, min(-lam[ind] / dlam[ind])) if np.any(ind) else 0.99\n        for j in range(biter):\n            xp = x + s * dx\n            lamp = lam + s * dlam\n            vp = v + s * dv\n            s *= beta\n            if all(xp > 0.0) and np.sqrt(\n                    norm(1.0 - lamp + AT.dot(vp)) ** 2 + norm(lamp * xp - oot) ** 2 + norm(A.dot(xp) - b) ** 2) <= (\n                    1.0 - alpha * s) * resnorm:\n                break\n        else:\n            break\n\n        eta = np.inner(lam, x)\n        rp = A.dot(xp) - b\n        rd = 1.0 - lamp + AT.dot(vp)\n        if max(eta, norm(rp), norm(rd)) < tol:\n            return x\n        x = xp\n        lam = lamp\n        v = vp\n        t = mu * d / eta\n\n    return x\n\n\n# NOTE: Ported to Python from l1-MAGIC: Cand\\'es & Romberg, ``l_1-MAGIC: Recovery of Sparse Signals via Convex Programming,",
    "Experiment Result": "Hyper-parameter search algorithm: TPE\nHyper-parameter scheduling algorithm for early stopping: Hyperband\nNumber of hyper-parameter sets to try: 20\nMetric to optimize: mean_accuracy, mode: max\nSearch space:\n- Learning rate: uniform distribution between 0.001 and 0.01\n- Optimizer: choice between 'sgd' and 'adam'\n- Training batch size: choice between 20, 32, and 64\nResources per trial: 0.5 GPU, unspecified CPU (default 1 CPU)\nFinal training (after HPO): True\n\nCore gradient-based subset selection (GSS) algorithm settings:\n- DSS type: GradMatchPB-Warm\n- Fraction of dataset selected: 0.1\n- Selection frequency: every 20 epochs/iterations\n- Warm-starting parameter: kappa=0.5 (meaning warm-starting is active)\n- Selection type: PerBatch (subset of mini-batches)\n- Linear layer used for gradients: True\n- Regularization constant (lam): 0\n- Epsilon for OMP solver (eps): 1e-100\n\nDataset: CIFAR-10, image type\nModel architecture: ResNet18\nLoss function: CrossEntropyLoss (reduction='none' for DSS, default for training)\nOptimizer (for model training): SGD, momentum=0.9, learning rate=0.05, weight_decay=5e-4, nesterov=True\nScheduler (for model training): Cosine Annealing, T_max=300 epochs\nTraining epochs: 300\nDevice: cuda"

    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper presents Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning (BOIL), an approach for efficient hyperparameter tuning in deep learning (DL) and deep reinforcement learning (DRL) systems. The core problem addressed is the high computational cost and inefficiency of traditional tuning methods that ignore intermediate training progress. BOIL proposes to learn an evaluation function that compresses the entire learning curve into a single numeric score based on training success and stability, rather than relying solely on final performance. It balances the benefit of additional training steps against their computational cost and enhances efficiency through a data augmentation technique that selectively includes intermediate training scores. The algorithm significantly outperforms existing baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time.",
    "Methodology": "BOIL models the cost-sensitive black-box function, which considers hyperparameters and training iterations, using a Gaussian Process (GP) with a product kernel. The cost function is approximated by a linear regressor. An acquisition function, a modified Expected Improvement criterion, is used to select the next evaluation point by balancing function value, uncertainty, and cost. A key component is the training curve compression, where a Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function is used to transform the entire learning curve into a single utility score, with its growth and middle point parameters learned directly from data by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood. To improve sample efficiency and avoid ill-conditioning, an active learning-based data augmentation technique selectively adds intermediate observations to the GP model, choosing samples at maximum predictive uncertainty, while adaptively controlling the number of samples to maintain a well-conditioned covariance matrix.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithm's efficiency was demonstrated by tuning hyperparameters for two DRL agents (Dueling DQN and A2C) across three environments (CartPole-v0, InvertedPendulum-v2, and Reacher-v2) and a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) on two datasets (SVHN and CIFAR10). All experimental results were averaged over 20 independent runs with different random seeds. Experiments were executed on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using tensorflow-gpu. DRL environments were from OpenAI gym and Mujoco, with implementations based on OpenAI Baselines. The GP models used square-exponential kernels, with parameters estimated by maximizing marginal likelihood. The maximum number of augmented points was set to 15, and the threshold for the natural log of the GP condition number was 20. Baselines included Hyperband and a continuous multi-task/fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO). Ablation studies compared BOIL to vanilla BO and BO with compression (BO-L).",
    "Limitations": "The approximation of the cost function c(x,t) by a linear regressor might be suboptimal, and employing a second GP or different parametric model could be more appropriate if the cost has a more complex dependency. Naive data augmentation by adding a full curve of points can lead to redundant information and severe ill-conditioning issues in the GP covariance matrix, especially in noisy DRL settings (though BOIL addresses this by selective sampling). Additionally, traditional stopping criteria, like the exponential decay assumed in Freeze-thaw BO, may not be applicable in DRL due to the unpredictable and noisy fluctuations of reward curves.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The proposed approach is not limited to machine learning algorithms but can be applied more generally to any process exhibiting an iterative structure. Potential applications include the optimization of manufacturing pipelines where factory settings are iteratively adjusted to increase productivity. The work also contributes to the broader goal of constructing automated pipelines for ML model training and deployment, encouraging practitioners to consider supervision and override mechanisms, and to reflect on potential biases and interpretability issues as models become more opaque due to automation.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nfrom bayes_opt.acquisition_functions import AcquisitionFunction, unique_rows\nfrom bayes_opt import ProductGaussianProcess\nfrom =0===============================================================================\")\n       \n            return acquisition_function_value*(-1)\n        \n        \n        if len(x)==self.dim:\n (self,SearchSpace,gp_hyper=None,logistic_hyper=None,verbose=0):\n        self.noise_delta=5e-4\n        self.noise_upperbound=1e-2\n        self.mycov=self.cov_RBF_time\n    Euc_dist=euclidean_distances(x1,x2)\n        exp_dist_x=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/lengthscale)\n        \n        Euc_dist=euclidean_distances(t1,t2)\n        exp_dist_t=np.exp(-np.square(Euc_dist)/lengthscale_t)\n        \n        return exp_dist_x*exp_dist_t\n                \n    def fit(self,X,T,Y,Y_curves):\n        temp=np.hstack((X,T))\n        ur = unique_rows(temp)\n        \n        T=T[ur]\n        X=X[ur]\n        Y=Y[ur]\n        \n        self.X=X\n        self.Y=Y\n        self.T=T\n        self.Y_curves=[val for idx,val in enumerate(Y_curves) if ur[idx]==True]\n        \n        for curves in self.Y_curves:\n            self.MaxEpisode=max(len(curves),self.MaxEpisode)\n            \n          -np.square(Euc_dist_t)/self.hyper['lengthscale_t'])+np.eye(len(X))*self.noise_delta\n          \n        if np.isnan(self.KK_x_x).any(): \n            print(\"nan in KK_x_x\")\n        \n  \n    def compute_condition_number(self):\n        cond_num=np.linalg.cond(self.KK_x_x)\n        return cond_num\n    \n\n    def log_marginal_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(self,hyper,noise_delta):\n\n        def compute_log_marginal_with_logistic_hyper(lengthscale, lengthscale_t,midpoint,growth,noise_delta):\n            temp=np.hstack((self.X,self.T))\n            ur = unique_rows(temp)\n +np.eye(len(myX))*noise_delta\n                    \n            \n            try:\n                temp_inv=np.linalg.solve(KK,myY)\n            except: \n                return -np.inf\n            \n            try:\n                first_term=-0.5*np.dot(myY.T,temp_inv)\n                \n                if KK.shape[0]>200:\n                    idx=np.random.permutation(KK.shape[0])\n                    idx=idx[:200]\n                    KK=KK[np.ix_(idx,idx)]\n                chol  = spla.cholesky(KK, lower=True)\n                W_logdet=np.sum(np.log(np.diag(chol)))\n    \n                second_term=-W_logdet\n            except: \n                return -np.inf\n            \n\n            logmarginal=first_term+second_term-0.5*len(myY)*np.log(2*3.14)\n                \n lengthscale,lengthscale_t,np.asscalar(first_term),np.asscalar(second_term)))\n\n            return np.asscalar(logmarginal)\n        \n        logmarginal=0\n\n        if not isinstance(hyper,list) and len(hyper.shape)==2:\n            logmarginal=[0]*hyper.shape[0]\n            growth=hyper[:,3]\n            midpoint=hyper[:,2]\n            lengthscale_t=hyper[:,1]\n = res\n            else:\n                x_max = res.x\n            max_log_marginal = val\n\n        return x_max\n\n    def optimize_lengthscale_logistic_hyper(self,prev_hyper,noise_delta):\n\n @staticmethod\n    def _mu(x, gp):\n        mean, var = gp.predict(x, eval_MSE=True)\n        mean=np.atleast_2d(mean).T\n        return mean\n                \n    @staticmethod\n    def _lcb(x, @staticmethod\n    def _ei(x, gp, y_max):\n        y_max=np.asscalar(y_max)\n        mean, var = gp.predict(x, eval_MSE=True)\n        var2 = np.maximum(var, 1e-10 + 0 * var)\n        z = (mean -
